# Memorandum 003 — Two U-2S Dragon Lady and four RQ-4B Global Hawk Block 40 high-altitude reconnaissance aircraft

*COMUSMACV to CINCPAC · received 2026-11-23 · three parts*

# Memorandum 003 — Part 1 of 3 · §1 Provision dossier


*COMUSMACV to CINCPAC · Saigon*

The provision gives me two U-2S Dragon Lady and four RQ-4B Global Hawk, and I have filed them as two provisions because they are two different instruments. The U-2S is a manned aircraft that flies above 70,000 feet with a radar, a camera and a signals intelligence payload; it is a deep look, sortie by sortie. The RQ-4B Block 40 is an unmanned aircraft that flies at 60,000 feet for more than thirty hours with a moving-target radar; it is a stare. The message gives no date, so both arrive on the campaign's opening date, 1 January 1964 [U: arrival defaults to campaign_opens]. Both are 2026 configurations: the U-2S as Air Combat Command flies it today, and the RQ-4B in the only block the Air Force still operates¹².

I have to state at the outset how I read the word "granted" for aircraft built to work inside a world of satellites, because the answer decides whether one of these provisions flies at all. The 2026 RQ-4 navigates by an inertial system aided by GPS, lands on differential GPS corrections from its own launch station, and is controlled beyond the horizon through a military X-band satellite link³. In January 1964 there is no GPS, and there will be none for fourteen years; there are no military communications satellites in orbit. Standing Rule A6 says that aerial ISR I am given works as specified, and I read that to mean the aircraft and sensors arrive able to do what their specifications say. I do not read it to mean that a satellite constellation arrives with them. My reading, therefore, is that each RQ-4 is delivered with what it minimally needs to take off, navigate and land without satellites — a ground-based local positioning array at its home runway to stand in for differential GPS, and an inertial navigation solution corrected in flight against radar imagery of surveyed ground — and that its control and its data travel only by line-of-sight link⁴. If that reading is wrong, and the RQ-4s arrive exactly as they fly from Grand Forks, they cannot land and should not be launched. The U-2S has no such problem: it has a pilot, an inertial system and a runway he can see.

These are the first provisions in this war that are machines, and the first that address the main-force half of the find-and-fix problem. The land programme, the Commission Against Corruption, the police and the province chiefs work on the other half — the infrastructure inside the hamlet⁵⁶. Nothing that flies at 60,000 feet can tell me who the tax collector is in a hamlet in Long An. I say that now, once, so that nothing that follows is read as a claim on it.

## §1.1 Lockheed Martin U-2S Dragon Lady (Block 20), 2026 configuration — P003a

### D1 · Designation, manufacturer, in-service date, quantity, unit organisation

**Designation.** Lockheed Martin U-2S Dragon Lady, Block 20, in its 2026 configuration: an ASARS-2A synthetic aperture radar, a SYERS-2C multispectral electro-optical and infrared camera, and the ASIP signals intelligence payload, with the glass cockpit, digital autopilot and modernised electronic warfare system of Block 20 and the avionics refresh of 2023. The aircraft is the large U-2R airframe, first flown in 1967 and reopened for production as the TR-1 in 1981, re-engined from 1994 with the GE F118-GE-101 turbofan and redesignated U-2S¹.

**Manufacturer and contractors.** Lockheed Martin for the airframe; Raytheon for the ASARS-2A radar; Northrop Grumman for ASIP; UTC Aerospace, now Collins, for SYERS and the optical bar camera¹.

**In-service date.** U-2S from the re-engining programme of 1994 onward. The 2026 fleet is 27 U-2S and 4 two-seat TU-2S, based at Beale AFB in California with forward detachments at Osan, Akrotiri and Fairford. Air Combat Command has proposed retiring the whole fleet more than once since 2024, and as of mid-2026 Congress was resisting it¹. Two aircraft is about one in fourteen of the world's remaining U-2S.

**Quantity.** Two aircraft⁷. No two-seat trainer is provided; pilot currency and any new pilots must come from outside the theater.

**What they join.** On the opening date the Strategic Air Command already has orders to put U-2s over South Vietnam. President Johnson directed U-2 reconnaissance in support of South Vietnam on 31 December 1963, and three U-2E aircraft of the 4028th Strategic Reconnaissance Squadron arrived at Bien Hoa as Operating Location 20 on 5 March 1964, after the Philippine government refused to let them stay at Clark⁸. Those three U-2Es carried film cameras and a modest SIGINT fit; their film came home in the aircraft and was processed after landing. The two U-2S arrive into that arrangement two months early, and I organise them as a detachment of the same Operating Location: a Strategic Air Command reconnaissance unit under the operational control of CINCPAC, tasked through MACV J-2 for South Vietnam and Laos and through CINCPAC for the North⁹.

**Unit organisation in theater.** One detachment, two aircraft, at Bien Hoa, with the ground exploitation station beside the runway and a second, smaller line-of-sight ground terminal at Da Nang to receive data from northern tracks¹⁰. The detachment commander is a lieutenant colonel; he has four to five pilots, a mobile-officer cell (the pilot in the chase car who talks every U-2 down to its landing), life-support technicians for the full pressure suits, maintainers and contractor field engineers¹¹.

### D2 · Crew and complement; personnel spaces against the ceiling

**Crew per platform.** One pilot in a full pressure suit¹. The U-2 is among the most demanding aircraft in the inventory to land: it has bicycle landing gear, outrigger "pogo" wheels that drop away on take-off, and a wing that wants to keep flying in ground effect, and every landing is talked down by a second U-2 pilot driving a chase car along the runway. So every sortie consumes two pilots' days, and a pilot who flies a nine-hour sortie in a pressure suit does not fly the next day¹².

**Detachment complement.** About 220 people in all¹³. The exploitation cell is the largest single element and the one most often forgotten: imagery analysts to read the radar and electro-optical take, SIGINT analysts and Vietnamese- and Chinese-language linguists to read the intercepts, and the communications technicians who run the datalink and the ground station¹⁴.

**Personnel spaces against the ceiling.** CONFIG sets no troop ceiling, so these 220 spaces foreclose nothing by number. They are still costly, and in a particular way. U-2 pilots are a small population — the 2026 Air Force trains them a handful at a time — and this detachment needs about one in eight of them¹⁵. The linguists are scarcer still. In January 1964 the United States has very few Vietnamese-language SIGINT linguists at all, and a 2026 detachment's linguists, if they come with it, are among the most valuable people in the theater, worth more doing ground and airborne direction-finding work than listening to the U-2's take alone.

### D3 · Weapons and payload

The U-2S carries no weapons. Its payload is three sensors, any two or all three of which can be flown on one sortie depending on bay configuration¹¹⁶.

**ASARS-2A synthetic aperture radar** (Raytheon). A multimode, real-time, high-resolution radar with search and spotlight imaging modes that detects and locates stationary and moving ground targets in all weather, day and night, from long stand-off range. Hughes developed it in the early 1980s; the ASARS Improvement Program delivered the 2A version from 2001 with more onboard processing and a faster datalink¹⁷. The ASARS-2B, with an active electronically scanned array and nearly double the range, is under contract to 2027 and is not part of this provision¹⁸.

**SYERS-2C** (Collins Aerospace). A long-focal-length electro-optical and infrared camera, multispectral in its later versions, that produces near-real-time imagery at long oblique ranges¹. It is a daylight, fair-weather sensor in its visible bands; its infrared and multispectral bands extend it somewhat into night, fog and haze¹⁹.

**ASIP, the Airborne Signals Intelligence Payload** (Northrop Grumman). A wideband communications and electronic intelligence receiver suite, with direction-finding¹²⁰.

**What is not provided.** The optical bar camera can still be mounted, but U-2 optical bar operations from Beale ended in 2022¹, and I do not assume the film processing chain for it exists in this provision. No air-refuelling capability exists²¹.

### D4 · Sensors: triple canopy, night, monsoon

This is the field that decides the provision's worth, so I take each sensor against the three conditions that define this theater.

**ASARS-2A against canopy.** A microwave radar sees the top of the forest, not what is under it. Radar that penetrates a forest canopy has to work below 1 GHz, at VHF and UHF²², and ASARS-2A is an X-band imaging radar built for resolution, not foliage penetration²³. Under the triple canopy of War Zone D, the Do Xa, the A Shau valley or the Laotian panhandle, it sees trees. It sees the clearings, the rice fields cut into the forest, the river crossings, the roads where the canopy is broken, the new tracks bulldozed by engineers, the bomb craters, the vehicle parks that are not under trees, and the changes in all of these between two passes²⁴. That is a great deal of information about a base area, and almost none about the men in it.

**ASARS-2A at night and in monsoon.** Darkness and cloud make no difference to it²¹. This matters more than anything else on this page, because between May and September over the South and Laos, and between October and March over the North and the coastal provinces of I Corps, the cameras that the 1964 force depends on are often blind for days.

**ASARS-2A moving targets.** In its moving-target mode it detects vehicles and boats moving faster than walking pace on roads, rivers and canals in the open¹⁸²⁵. Men on foot under canopy it does not see.

**Detection ranges.** No published figure for ASARS-2A's range exists. Open descriptions say "long stand-off" and that the 2B nearly doubles it¹⁸. I estimate that from 70,000 feet ASARS-2A produces search-mode imagery out to roughly 100 nautical miles slant range and spotlight imagery at somewhat shorter range²⁶. Against target types: a truck on an open road is detectable in search mode at that range; a 37mm gun position in the open is identifiable in spotlight; a bunker under canopy is not detectable at any range²⁷.

**SYERS-2C against canopy, night and monsoon.** A camera sees no further into the forest than the human eye, though its infrared bands show heat and its multispectral bands can distinguish cut vegetation used as camouflage from living foliage²⁸. At night its infrared band sees fires, engines and warm vehicles in the open. In monsoon cloud it sees nothing¹⁹.

**ASIP against canopy, night and monsoon.** Radio signals ignore canopy, darkness and cloud. From 70,000 feet the radio horizon is about 325 nautical miles, so the U-2 can hear a VHF transmitter anywhere in South Vietnam south of Hue from a track over the coast near Phan Thiet (radio horizon ≈ 1.23 × √70,000 ft). This is the one sensor on either aircraft that addresses the enemy under the canopy, because the PAVN and VC main force talk on radio at regimental level and above, and the B-2 Front, the regional commands and the infiltration stations on the Trail send traffic by HF. How much the 1964 enemy transmits is the limit, and he transmits little by our standards; most of his traffic below regiment goes by courier.

**Which half of find-and-fix.** The main-force half, entirely. The U-2 tells me where base areas, trails, crossings, depots and headquarters are, and by direction-finding roughly where a regimental or front transmitter is. It tells me nothing about the infrastructure inside the population, whose members do not use radios and do not live under the canopy.

### D5 · Performance

**Altitude.** Above 70,000 feet¹. The U-2R generation normally operated above 65,000 feet²¹.

**Speed.** About 410 mph, or 355 knots¹.

**Range and endurance.** Ferry range more than 7,000 miles. Endurance more than 12 hours, but missions normally limited to about 9 hours by the pilot in his pressure suit; a 14-hour endurance sortie was flown on 31 July 2025 as a record¹. The U-2 cannot be refuelled in the air²¹.

**Engine and airframe.** One GE F118-GE-101 turbofan of 17,000 lb thrust; wingspan 105 feet, length 63 feet, height 16 feet, maximum take-off weight 40,000 lb¹.

**Sortie rate.** With two aircraft, five pilots and one ground station, the detachment can sustain about one nine-hour sortie a day, about 25 a month, and surge to two a day for a few days²⁹.

**Coverage per sortie.** A nine-hour sortie flies about 3,000 nautical miles of track (355 knots × about 8.5 hours on track). With a search swath I estimate at 60–100 nautical miles wide for ASARS-2A, one sortie can image something on the order of 100,000 square nautical miles in search mode, while also taking a few hundred spotlight images of named targets³⁰. South Vietnam is about 50,000 square nautical miles. In principle the whole of the South, the Laotian panhandle and the North's southern provinces can be imaged in radar once a day or two; in practice tasking spends most of each sortie on spotlight targets.

**Protection.** None in armour. The U-2's protection is altitude. Above 70,000 feet it is out of reach of every gun in the theater: the 37mm and 57mm guns that line the Trail by 1965 are effective to about 3,000 and 6,000 metres, and the 85mm and 100mm guns of the North to perhaps 9,000–12,000 metres³¹. The MiG-17, which arrives at Phuc Yen from China in August 1964, cannot reach it. The MiG-21, in combat over the North from 1966, can zoom-climb toward it but not hold an intercept there³². The SA-2 can reach it, as it did over Sverdlovsk in 1960 and Cuba in 1962. The Block 20 aircraft carries a modern self-protection suite against SA-2-era radars¹³³.

### D6 · Communications, datalink, and talking to ARVN

**Architecture.** The U-2S sends radar, electro-optical and SIGINT data down a wideband line-of-sight datalink to a ground station while it is "on tether", within about 220 miles of the station; beyond that it stores the data and transmits it when it comes back within range²¹. Its 2026 configuration also carries Link 16 and the new datalinks of its modernisation programme¹. A 2026 U-2 in the Middle East would also pass data beyond line of sight by satellite; in 1964 it cannot, because no satellite exists to carry it.

**What the geometry means in Vietnam.** A ground station at Bien Hoa holds the aircraft on tether out to roughly Pleiku and Qui Nhon in the north and over the Cambodian base areas from the Fishhook to the Parrot's Beak, and to Sihanoukville at the edge of range³⁴. A second terminal at Da Nang covers I Corps, the DMZ, the Laotian panhandle from Tchepone to the Mu Gia pass and the North's southern provinces to about Vinh³⁵. Hanoi and Haiphong, about 340 nautical miles from Da Nang, are out of tether: tracks over the Red River delta are stored and downloaded on the way home, so northern collection arrives hours after the event, not minutes³⁶.

**Inside MACV.** The ground station's products go by secure landline and teletype to MACV J-2 at Tan Son Nhut and to the Seventh Air Force (still the 2nd Air Division in January 1964). The 2026 station produces digital imagery and formatted reports; the 1964 headquarters that receives them has teletype, courier and photographic prints. So the station must make paper as well as files. A target folder for a strike in the North can be in Saigon by evening. A report that a column of trucks crossed the Ban Karai pass at 0300 reaches a division G-2 in II Corps the next day unless someone builds the channel to carry it faster.

**Talking to ARVN.** The aircraft cannot and should not. Its products are classified at levels the Joint General Staff will not be cleared for, and the Joint General Staff is penetrated by the enemy. What ARVN receives is a sanitised product: a grid reference, a description, a time, with no indication of how it was obtained, passed through the US advisory chain. The SIGINT especially must never be attributed, because the first sign that we read his radio is the last good day we have reading it.

### D7 · Domain conditions

**Basing.** Bien Hoa, the historical home of OL-20. The U-2 needs a long, smooth, well-maintained hard runway, generous taxiways, and above all a clear runway centreline for its bicycle-gear landings; Bien Hoa's concrete runway serves³⁷. Da Nang serves as a recovery alternate. Takhli in Thailand, from which the CIA's U-2s flew over the North and China until April 1964, is the natural second base for northern tracks if the Thai government permits⁸.

**Revetment and hardening.** The U-2 must be kept in a hangar or a covered, revetted shelter. On 1 November 1964 the VC mortared Bien Hoa from outside the perimeter and destroyed five B-57 bombers on the ramp, killing four Americans. A U-2 caught on that ramp is a loss I cannot replace. The detachment needs two hardened shelters, a separate perimeter, and patrolling of the mortar belt around the base before the aircraft are exposed³⁸.

**Survivability over the South.** Total. Small arms and 12.7mm machine guns reach about 1,500 metres³⁹; the U-2 flies at more than 21,000 metres (70,000 ft ≈ 21,300 m).

**Survivability over the Trail.** Total against the guns at every date the record is likely to reach⁴⁰. The risk over Laos is an engine failure and a pilot ejecting into the panhandle, not a shootdown.

**Survivability over the North.** Safe until the SA-2 arrives. The first SA-2 sites near Hanoi are photographed on 5 April 1965 and the first American aircraft falls to one on 24 July 1965. From then on, the U-2 flies over the North only along routes planned around known sites, and accepts a real risk on every penetration of the Hanoi and Haiphong defences; its self-protection suite reduces that risk but does not remove it⁴¹.

**Survivability near China.** China shot down several Nationalist U-2s over its territory in the 1960s. Tracks along the Chinese border in 1964 are safe in practice from Chinese fighters, but an SA-2 in southern China would be a different matter, and a U-2 down in China is a political event of the first order⁴².

### D8 · Logistics footprint

**Fuel.** The U-2S burns a special low-volatility thermally stable jet fuel, JPTS, which does not freeze or boil off at 70,000 feet. JPTS is not stocked anywhere in Southeast Asia in 1964. It must come by tanker ship or by drums through Saigon and be stored separately⁴³. At about one sortie a day the detachment burns roughly 2,500–3,000 US gallons a day⁴⁴.

**Spares.** Under A2, the detachment has modern maintenance and its spares at its base. Each part comes from a 2026 depot through a supply chain that exists only by stipulation; nothing for the U-2S is manufactured anywhere in 1964. The two most sensitive items are the engine and the sensor line-replaceable units, both of which need contractor engineers to change and test.

**Specialist personnel.** Pressure-suit technicians; Lockheed, Raytheon, Northrop and Collins field engineers; ground-station technicians; imagery and SIGINT analysts; linguists⁴⁵.

**Sustainment rate.** About 25 sorties a month, 200–250 flying hours a month across the two aircraft (D5's sortie rate).

**Cost.** Hourly operating cost is not published in a source I consulted. I estimate it at US$30,000–40,000 per flying hour at 2026 prices, so about US$7–10 million a month at the rates above⁴⁶.

**What RVNAF cannot supply.** All of it. The Vietnamese Air Force cannot maintain, fly, fuel or exploit the U-2, and nothing in its future can be made to.

### D9 · Transferability to ARVN

**None.** The aircraft, the sensors and the take are among the most sensitive things the United States owns. The skills to fly a U-2 take a qualified fighter or multi-engine pilot about a year of conversion⁴⁷. The Vietnamese Air Force of 1964 has under 400 pilots, flying A-1H Skyraiders, T-28s and C-47s, and its best are needed in them. Transfer would also hand the most sensitive American technology to the most penetrated institution in the Republic.

**What can be transferred.** The product, sanitised, through the advisory chain, as described in D6; and in time a Vietnamese imagery-interpretation cell trained to read declassified radar prints and maps derived from them⁴⁸. Under Vietnamization the aircraft leave with the Americans. The Republic inherits maps.

### D10 · Employment envelope

**Performs to specification:** Radar imaging and moving-target search over open terrain, roads, rivers, the coast and the North's transport system, in any weather, day or night, on tether to a ground station. SIGINT collection against an enemy who uses his radios, at ranges no ground station can match. Electro-optical imaging in clear daylight.

**Performance collapses:** Against anything under canopy, for radar and camera alike. Against a disciplined enemy who keeps radio silence; his main force goes off the air before every major operation. For SYERS, in monsoon cloud. Over the North once SA-2s are emplaced along the route, where every sortie carries risk. Beyond line of sight, where real-time becomes after-the-fact. And at the tempo of daily operations sustained over months, which two aircraft can meet only if neither is lost or down for long.

**The EW environment.** The North's early-warning radars will track a U-2 from the moment it crosses the coast; they cannot reach it, but they will tell Hanoi where it is looking⁴⁹. Its datalink is not jammed by anything the enemy owns in this period⁵⁰.

**Radius from base.** On tether, about 220 miles from Bien Hoa or Da Nang²¹. Off tether, anywhere within roughly 1,500 miles (a 9-hour sortie at 355 knots, with reserves).

**Tempo over weeks.** One sortie a day is sustainable for months if both aircraft remain serviceable; with one aircraft down, one sortie every other day⁵¹.

### D11 · Failure modes

**What breaks first.** The aircraft, and the pilot's day. A U-2 with an engine or flight-control problem is down for days waiting for a part and an engineer⁵⁰. A crosswind or a wet runway at Bien Hoa cancels a landing, and a monsoon squall over the base cancels a launch; the U-2 is notoriously sensitive to crosswinds on landing.

**Partial failure.** One aircraft unserviceable halves the sortie rate and ends surge. A ground-station failure turns every sortie into store-and-forward, and products arrive the next day. A failed SIGINT payload leaves the radar working; a failed radar leaves the camera, which in the monsoon is useless.

**Total failure.** Loss of an aircraft over the North, an accident on landing, or a mortar round through a hangar. With two aircraft, one loss halves the provision for good; nothing replaces it.

**What the force does in the gap.** It returns to what it had: the U-2Es of OL-20 from March 1964, the Ryan 147 Lightning Bug drones from October 1964, the RF-101C detachment at Tan Son Nhut, the Army's OV-1 Mohawks with side-looking radar, and the Army's radio direction-finding aircraft⁸⁵².

### D12 · Degradation curve

**Month one.** The aircraft are new to the theater, the pilots new to its weather, the analysts new to the enemy. The first month produces superb imagery that nobody yet knows how to read: radar shadows in the canopy that might be bunkers or might be rock; a transmitter that might be a regimental headquarters or might be a relay station⁵⁰. Mission-capable rate high, about 85–90%, on fresh spares⁵³.

**Month twelve.** Analysts have built a library of change: which crossings are used in the dry season, which clearings are new, which transmitters move with which units. The product is far better than at month one. Mission-capable rate about 75–80% as the tropical climate works on seals, connectors and electronics⁵⁴. Pilot proficiency holds, because these are career U-2 pilots.

**What declines first.** The SIGINT take, because the enemy learns. The first time an American strike lands on a headquarters located by direction-finding, his signal discipline tightens, as it did historically after ARDF began to tell. Second, the electro-optical take, because the monsoon comes. Third, spares depth, if the supply chain from 2026 is slower than the stipulation assumes.

### D13 · Comparative benchmark

**Against the U-2E of OL-20.** The U-2E is the same high-altitude idea with 1950s sensors: film cameras whose take returns with the aircraft and must be processed before anyone reads it, and a SIGINT fit of the period. The U-2S has a radar that sees through cloud and darkness, a camera that downlinks while the aircraft is still overhead, and a modern SIGINT suite with direction-finding¹. In monsoon weather the U-2E produces nothing; the U-2S produces radar imagery every sortie. The margin is, at a guess, five to ten times the useful product per sortie in the dry season, and the difference between something and nothing in the wet⁵⁵.

**Against the RF-101C Voodoo and, from late 1965, the RF-4C Phantom.** Low- and medium-altitude photographic reconnaissance on film, fast, and with real risk over the Trail and the North. The U-2S covers in one sortie what an RF-101C covers in perhaps fifteen to twenty, without exposure to guns, but at lower resolution than a low-level pass and with no ability to look under the edge of the canopy the way a low oblique photograph can⁵⁶.

**Against the Ryan 147 Lightning Bug.** From 11 October 1964 the drones fly from Bien Hoa, launched from a DC-130 and recovered by parachute and a CH-3E helicopter; their high-altitude models reach about 62,500 feet with a U-2-type camera, and their losses over the war are 578 drones⁵². They are expendable; the U-2S is not. Per sortie, a U-2S with radar and SIGINT produces several times what a camera drone produces, and does so in weather⁵⁰.

**Against the Army's OV-1 Mohawk with the AN/APS-94 side-looking radar.** The Mohawk's radar has a moving-target mode and flies at low altitude, with a range of a few tens of kilometres⁵⁷. The U-2S sees several times further and from safety, but the Mohawk is under the command of the corps that uses its picture.

**Against the Army's radio direction-finding aircraft.** The 3rd Radio Research Unit's U-6A Beavers have flown airborne direction-finding since 1962. They fix transmitters to a few hundred metres; a single high-altitude U-2 fixes them to a few kilometres unless it builds a long baseline over the course of a sortie⁵⁸. The U-2 hears much more; the Beaver locates better. They are complements, and the best use of ASIP is to cue the Beavers.

### D14 · Negative specification
The U-2S cannot see under the canopy, with radar or camera, and the enemy's base camps, hospitals, depots and regiments are under the canopy. It cannot see the VC infrastructure in the hamlets at all; it addresses the main-force half of find-and-fix only. It does not provide real-time coverage beyond about 220 miles of a ground station; northern collection arrives after landing. Two aircraft give about one sortie a day, not persistent surveillance: it looks at a place, then goes away. Its SIGINT locates transmitters to kilometres, not metres, and only while the enemy transmits. Its camera is useless in monsoon cloud. It has no weapons, and its product is worth only what a strike, raid or airmobile force can do with it inside the hours the location stays true. Over the North it is not invulnerable once SA-2s arrive in 1965, and a loss cannot be replaced. Its products cannot safely be shared with ARVN except in sanitised form, because the Joint General Staff is penetrated.

### D15 · What it denies the enemy
The cover of weather and darkness over open ground: roads, rivers, fords, truck parks, depots and construction in the North, Laos and the base areas can no longer be hidden by cloud or night. Radio security at regiment and above: every transmission becomes a possible location. The surprise of a newly built road, airfield, SAM site or base area, which radar change detection reveals within days.

### D16 · Honest verdict
A large improvement in what MACV and the air campaign know about the enemy's rear — the Trail, the North's transport system, the base areas and the radio nets of his higher headquarters — at a modest cost in people and none in lives. It is the best strategic reconnaissance in the world in 1964 by a wide margin, and over the North it will make target selection and damage assessment honest and air losses lower. It does not find a regiment in the jungle tonight, it does not find the VCI, and two aircraft cannot watch anything continuously. Its value to the war in the South is as a cue — for the direction-finding aircraft, the long-range patrols, the Special Forces and the bombers — and its value depends on there being something fast enough to act on what it finds. Real, durable, and not decisive alone.

**Key figures — P003a**

- Quantity: 2 U-2S Block 20⁷, about one in fourteen of the 2026 fleet of 27 U-2S 
- Ceiling above 70,000 ft; speed about 410 mph; range 7,000+ miles; F118-GE-101 of 17,000 lb 
- Mission about 9 hours normally, 12+ possible; no air refuelling 
- Sensors: ASARS-2A radar (search, spotlight, moving targets, all weather), SYERS-2C EO/IR, ASIP SIGINT¹¹⁸
- Datalink: real time within about 220 miles of a ground station, stored beyond²¹; ground stations at Bien Hoa and Da Nang⁵⁹
- Sortie rate: about one a day, about 25 a month⁶⁰
- Coverage: on the order of 100,000 sq nm of radar search per sortie⁶¹
- Personnel: about 220⁶²
- Cost: about US$30,000–40,000 per flying hour⁶³
- Threats: immune to guns and MiG-17s; vulnerable to the SA-2 from April 1965 

## §1.2 Northrop Grumman RQ-4B Global Hawk Block 40, 2026 configuration — P003b

### D1 · Designation, manufacturer, in-service date, quantity, unit organisation

**Designation.** Northrop Grumman RQ-4B Global Hawk Block 40: a high-altitude, long-endurance, remotely piloted aircraft carrying the Multi-Platform Radar Technology Insertion Program radar, MP-RTIP, an active electronically scanned array that provides ground moving-target indication and synthetic aperture imagery³. The system is the aircraft, the Launch and Recovery Element that takes it off and lands it, and the Mission Control Element from which a pilot and a sensor operator fly the mission².

**Manufacturer.** Northrop Grumman for the aircraft and radar; Raytheon for the ground segment; L3Harris for communications²³.

**In-service date.** Global Hawk first flew on 28 February 1998; the Block 40 reached initial operational capability in August 2016. The Block 10 was retired in 2011, the Block 20 converted to communications relays, and the Block 30, which carried the imaging and SIGINT suite, retired in 2022⁶⁴. The 2026 Air Force fleet is nine Block 40s at Grand Forks AFB, with forward locations at Andersen, Sigonella, Fairford and Yokota; Congress has extended its retirement to at least 2030². Four aircraft is almost half of it.

**Quantity.** Four aircraft⁷, with one Launch and Recovery Element and one Mission Control Element⁶⁵.

**Configuration.** As stated at the head of this memorandum: without GPS or satellite links, each aircraft is read as arriving with a local positioning array at its home runway and an in-flight navigation update against radar imagery of surveyed ground, and all control and data go by line-of-sight link⁶⁶. I mark every place below where that reading matters.

**Unit organisation in theater.** One expeditionary reconnaissance squadron, four aircraft, under CINCPAC's operational control through the 2nd Air Division, tasked by MACV J-2⁶⁷. The Launch and Recovery Element and the Mission Control Element sit together at the home base, because the MCE can control the aircraft only by line of sight. A relay terminal at Da Nang extends the line-of-sight control and data link to the north⁶⁸.

### D2 · Crew and complement; personnel spaces against the ceiling

**Crew per platform.** None on board. Three people fly each mission: a Launch and Recovery Element pilot who takes the aircraft off and lands it, and a Mission Control Element pilot and sensor operator who fly the mission². A 30-hour sortie needs four to five shifts of the MCE crew (6–8-hour shifts).

**Squadron complement.** About 380 people in all⁶⁹. As with the U-2, the exploitation cell is the largest element. A moving-target radar watching a 24-hour orbit over the Laotian panhandle generates thousands of tracks a day, and someone has to decide which of them are trucks of the 559th Transportation Group and which are a Lao logging lorry⁷⁰.

**Personnel spaces against the ceiling.** No ceiling is set, so the 380 spaces foreclose nothing by number. They are 2026 specialists — remotely piloted aircraft pilots and sensor operators, radar analysts trained in GMTI exploitation — who do not exist anywhere in the 1964 force and cannot be trained by it. Replacements come only from the stipulated supply that delivered them.

### D3 · Weapons and payload

The RQ-4B carries no weapons. Its maximum payload is 3,000 lb², and in Block 40 that payload is one sensor.

**MP-RTIP radar** (Northrop Grumman and Raytheon). An active electronically scanned array that collects synthetic aperture imagery of stationary targets and tracks ground moving targets at the same time⁷¹. It provides wide-area surveillance of stationary and moving targets and has maritime modes for surface vessels³. It can also track low, slow aircraft and cruise missiles, which matters little in this war².

**What Block 40 does not carry.** No camera of any kind, and no SIGINT payload⁷¹. The imaging and SIGINT suite flew on the Block 30, which was retired in 2022². This is the line in the dossier most likely to be forgotten: the RQ-4 in this provision sees with radar and only with radar.

**Known deficiencies.** In its operational testing the Block 40's radar showed problems of SAR image quality and sensor stability that the Air Force addressed with software, and whose fixes were still not fully evaluated in 2014⁷¹. By 2026 the system has a decade of operational use², and I assume the problems are largely resolved, but the SAR imagery of the Block 40 is a by-product of a GMTI radar, not the equal of the U-2's ASARS-2A⁷².

### D4 · Sensors: triple canopy, night, monsoon

**Against canopy.** Like the ASARS-2A, the MP-RTIP is a microwave radar, and canopy defeats it²²⁷³. It does not see men or vehicles under trees. It sees them where the trees stop: at a ford, on a road through a clearing, crossing a rice field, moving along a canal or a river, or on the open road from Vinh to the Mu Gia pass⁵⁰. The Ho Chi Minh Trail was built to avoid exactly this exposure, under trellised canopy where the forest allowed. But even the Trail crosses rivers, climbs passes where the forest is thin, and leaves the trees where bombing has removed them.

**At night.** No difference⁶⁴. This is the sensor's great advantage in a war fought at night. A sampan convoy crossing the Plain of Reeds at 0200, a truck column on Route 15 north of the Mu Gia pass, a trawler standing in toward the coast of Phu Yen: all of them are as visible at night as at noon⁵⁰.

**In monsoon.** No difference⁶⁴. Heavy rain attenuates an X-band radar somewhat and reduces its range in a cell of intense rainfall, but the sensor keeps working through the monsoon in a way no camera does⁷⁴.

**Detection ranges and target types.** Published figures do not exist. I estimate that from 60,000 feet the MP-RTIP tracks moving vehicles and boats out to about 100–150 nautical miles, can observe an area of tens of thousands of square kilometres from one orbit, and can detect a man walking in open terrain at shorter range⁷⁵. Against target types: trucks on open roads, yes; boats on rivers and canals, yes; steel-hulled trawlers at sea, yes, at long range; a file of porters on a trail under canopy, no; a column of infantry crossing an open paddy at night, perhaps, if it moves together and fast enough⁷⁶.

**The discrimination problem.** GMTI sees movement, not identity. In the Mekong Delta at night, tens of thousands of sampans move on the canals, and most of them belong to farmers and traders⁷⁷. A radar track cannot tell a VC tax collector's sampan from a rice merchant's. This is the sensor's most dangerous property, and I return to it in D14.

**Which half of find-and-fix.** The main-force half — and, within it, only the part that moves in the open. It does not touch the infrastructure.

### D5 · Performance

**Altitude.** Ceiling 60,000 feet².

**Speed.** Maximum about 357 mph; cruise about 357 mph²³.

**Endurance and range.** More than 32 hours, with 24 hours on station at 1,200 miles from base; range about 14,150 miles².

**Engine and airframe.** One Rolls-Royce F137-RR-100 turbofan of 7,600 lb thrust; wingspan 130.9 feet, length 47.6 feet, height 15.3 feet, maximum take-off weight 32,250 lb, empty weight about 14,950 lb²³.

**Orbits.** From Bien Hoa, every station in the South is less than an hour's flying away (no point in RVN is more than about 350 nm from Bien Hoa). With a 30-hour sortie and an hour's transit, each sortie gives about 28 hours on station. Four aircraft, at a mission-capable rate of about 75%, can sustain one continuous 24-hour orbit indefinitely and a second orbit for about a third of the time, or two orbits for a week's surge⁷⁸.

**Coverage per orbit.** One orbit at 60,000 feet watches, at a time, an area of perhaps 20,000–40,000 square kilometres for moving targets⁷⁹. That is roughly the Laotian panhandle from the Mu Gia pass to Tchepone, or the whole of the Mekong Delta's southern provinces, or the coast of the II Corps provinces from Qui Nhon to Phan Rang out to 100 miles at sea⁸⁰.

**Protection.** None but altitude. At 60,000 feet it is beyond every gun and every MiG of the period⁸¹. It is well inside the SA-2's envelope, and it has no pilot to see a missile coming or to manoeuvre against it⁸².

### D6 · Communications, datalink, and talking to ARVN

**Architecture.** The 2026 RQ-4 is controlled and sends its data both by line of sight and by satellite⁷¹, the latter through a military X-band satellite link to the Mission Control Element. A common data link delivers imagery directly to ground stations in line of sight³. In 1964, only the line-of-sight links exist.

**What the geometry means.** The radio horizon from 60,000 feet is about 300 nautical miles (1.23 × √60,000 ft). A practical control and data link of about 200–250 nautical miles from a ground terminal is a fair estimate⁸³. A Mission Control Element at Bien Hoa controls the aircraft anywhere in the South from about Qui Nhon southward, over the whole Cambodian border, over Sihanoukville and the Gulf of Thailand coast⁸⁴. A relay terminal at Da Nang, linked to Bien Hoa by landline or microwave, extends control and data over I Corps, the DMZ, the Laotian panhandle and the North to about Vinh⁵⁰. The Red River delta is out of reach.

**What happens if the link is lost.** The Global Hawk is designed to fly a pre-programmed route on its own if the link fails, and to return home⁸⁵. Without GPS it navigates on its inertial system, drifting perhaps a nautical mile or more per hour without updates⁸⁶, and the radar map-matching of my reading corrects it. A link outage over Laos is an inconvenience. Over the North it is a gift to the enemy if the aircraft drifts into a defended area.

**Inside MACV.** Moving-target tracks are a live product: a dot moving on a map display, with history. Their value is in minutes. The ground station can display them, but the 1964 force has no network to carry them to anyone but people standing in the station. A forward air controller, a strike aircraft or an airborne command post can be talked onto a track by radio; a ground unit can be given a grid by voice⁵⁰. Every hop costs time.

**Talking to ARVN.** As with the U-2, never directly. An ARVN corps commander can be given a reported location, unattributed, through his adviser. The tracks themselves stay in American hands.

### D7 · Domain conditions

**Basing.** Bien Hoa as the main operating base, with the Mission Control Element and Launch and Recovery Element together; Da Nang for the relay terminal and as a recovery alternate⁸⁷. The aircraft needs a long hard runway and, on my reading, a surveyed local positioning array around it for automatic take-off and landing; the array is site-specific, so each runway the aircraft uses must have one⁸⁸. Bien Hoa is chosen over Tan Son Nhut because Tan Son Nhut is saturated with traffic, and over Da Nang because Bien Hoa sits under the middle of the coverage the aircraft is most needed for: the Delta, the Cambodian border and the coast.

**Revetment and hardening.** As for the U-2, the RQ-4 is a large, irreplaceable aircraft that must not stand on an open ramp. Its 131-foot wingspan needs a large shelter². The Bien Hoa mortar attack of 1 November 1964 is the warning. I need four shelters and a protected perimeter for an aircraft that has none of a fighter's protection⁵⁰.

**Survivability over the South.** Total against the enemy's weapons (60,000 ft ≈ 18,300 m against about 1,500 m for the 12.7mm).

**Survivability over the Trail.** Total against guns; the 100mm gun's ceiling is about 12,000–15,000 metres in the most generous estimate⁸⁹. SA-2s did not deploy into Laos until the last years of the war.

**Survivability over the North.** Low once SA-2s are emplaced in 1965. A slow, large, unmanoeuvrable aircraft at 60,000 feet is the SA-2's ideal target. Over the North after mid-1965, the RQ-4 can look only from outside the SA-2 rings, which with a radar of 100-mile reach still covers much of the North's southern provinces from offshore or from over Laos⁵⁰.

**Survivability near China and Cambodia.** Over Cambodia, a large unmanned aircraft is a violation of a neutral's airspace and a propaganda gift if lost⁵⁰. Standing off inside South Vietnamese airspace, the radar sees perhaps 150–200 km into Cambodia without overflight⁹⁰, so the base areas along the border can be watched without crossing it. Near China, I do not fly it.

**Accident risk.** The Global Hawk programme lost four of its early prototypes and has had crashes throughout its service, including in 2018, 2021 and 2022²³. In a tropical theater, without GPS, from runways the aircraft was not designed for, the accident rate will be higher than in peacetime operations from Grand Forks⁵⁰. Over a war of several years, the loss of one or two of the four aircraft to accident is more likely than not⁹¹.

### D8 · Logistics footprint

**Fuel.** Standard JP-8 jet fuel, the kerosene that every jet in theater burns in a near equivalent⁹². A 30-hour sortie burns roughly 2,000–2,500 US gallons⁹³. At one continuous orbit, about 2,000 US gallons a day.

**Spares and maintenance.** Contractor logistics support is about half the RQ-4's hourly cost³, which tells me who keeps it flying: Northrop Grumman and Raytheon field service engineers. They come with the provision⁹⁴.

**Cost.** About US$18,900 per flight hour in 2013 dollars. At one continuous orbit, about 730 flight hours a month, that is about US$14 million a month at 2013 prices. Each aircraft is a flyaway cost of about US$130 million at 2013 prices³.

**What RVNAF cannot supply.** Everything. The Republic has no fuel standard, no runway equipment, no maintenance capacity and no exploitation capacity for this aircraft, and never will.

### D9 · Transferability to ARVN

**None.** The aircraft cannot be operated without 2026 training and support that has no equivalent in the 1960s or 1970s. A Vietnamese crew could be trained, in principle, in about a year, but the aircraft cannot be maintained without contractor engineers from 2026, and the product is so sensitive and the Republic's services so penetrated that handing them a live picture of what the United States can see would cost more than it gains⁵⁰.

**What can be transferred.** Sanitised reports. Under Vietnamization, the aircraft leave with the Americans, and what the Republic keeps is whatever its own forces learned to do with American reports in the meantime.

### D10 · Employment envelope

**Performs to specification:** Persistent moving-target surveillance of open terrain, roads, rivers, canals and the sea, day or night, in any weather, from a station held for 24 hours at a time, inside about 250 nautical miles of a ground terminal⁶⁸. Radar imagery of fixed targets in the same area, of lower quality than ASARS-2A.

**Performance collapses:** Under canopy. Against dismounts moving slowly or in small groups. Where the enemy is mixed into a civilian population whose movement looks the same. Beyond line of sight. Over the North after the SA-2 arrives. When the link drops. In typhoons that close the base, and in the occasional extreme rainfall cell⁵⁰.

**Tempo over weeks.** One continuous orbit is sustainable for months, if the four aircraft survive. A second continuous orbit is not⁵¹.

**EW environment.** The enemy has no jammer capable of affecting the datalink or the radar in this period⁵⁰. He has radar to see the aircraft, and once he understands what it is, he will learn its pattern.

### D11 · Failure modes

**What breaks first.** The aircraft on the ground, from tropical heat and humidity in electronics designed for Grand Forks⁵⁰. Then the link, when a terminal goes down or the aircraft flies beyond range. Then navigation, if the local positioning array at Bien Hoa is damaged by a mortar round or a power cut; with no fallback, take-off and landing at that runway stop until it is repaired⁸⁸.

**Partial failure.** Three aircraft give one orbit with no margin for maintenance; two give one orbit with gaps of a day or more. A radar that drops its SAR mode still tracks moving targets. A broken exploitation chain leaves the station with a picture no one can act on.

**Total failure.** Loss of the ground segment at Bien Hoa, to an attack or a fire, grounds all four aircraft until it is replaced; there is only one⁵⁰. Loss of an aircraft in an accident or to an SA-2 reduces the force by a quarter for good.

**What the force does in the gap.** What it did historically: the OV-1 Mohawk's side-looking radar by corps, U-2Es and drones, and, from 1967, the seismic and acoustic sensors of Igloo White on the Trail.

### D12 · Degradation curve

**Month one.** The radar works; the analysts do not yet know the theater. They see thousands of tracks a night and cannot tell which matter. Mission-capable rate about 80%⁹⁵.

**Month twelve.** The analysts have built pattern-of-life for the roads, rivers and coast in their orbits: when the Delta's sampans normally move, what a convoy looks like on Route 15 in the dry season, what a trawler looks like among the junks off Phu Yen. The product is much more useful than at month one. Mission-capable rate about 70–75%⁹⁶. One aircraft may already have been lost⁹⁷.

**What declines first.** The enemy's exposure, as he learns. A truck column that is struck in the open within the hour learns to move in rain under canopy, in small groups, in the gaps of the orbit [H: the enemy's historical adaptation to Igloo White and the gunships]. The second thing is the navigation reading: an array that is down for a week grounds the aircraft for a week.

### D13 · Comparative benchmark

**Against the OV-1 Mohawk with AN/APS-94 side-looking radar.** The Mohawk is the only airborne moving-target radar in theater in 1964. It flies at low altitude for a few hours and sees a few tens of kilometres⁵⁷. The RQ-4 sees perhaps five times further, from beyond the reach of any gun, for thirty hours at a time⁵⁰. The margin in area watched continuously is on the order of fifty times per aircraft⁹⁸.

**Against Igloo White** (from 1967 historically). The seismic and acoustic sensors on the Trail detected trucks and men under canopy, where they were placed, and needed an EC-121 relay and a large infiltration surveillance centre at Nakhon Phanom. The RQ-4 sees nothing under canopy but sees everything in the open over a whole sector. They are complements, not rivals: one hears the Trail where it hides, the other sees it where it shows.

**Against the U-2S of this memorandum.** The U-2S sees farther and better in imagery and has SIGINT; it looks and goes away. The RQ-4 sees moving things continuously. The U-2 builds the map; the RQ-4 watches the traffic on it.

**Against the JSTARS of later wars.** The E-8C JSTARS of Desert Storm watched large armoured forces moving on open desert. Here the enemy is an infantry force that moves on foot under canopy, and the comparison flatters the RQ-4: in 1991 a moving-target radar found an enemy army on the move; here it will find mostly trucks, boats and civilians.

### D14 · Negative specification
The RQ-4B Block 40 carries a radar and nothing else: no camera, no infrared, no SIGINT. It cannot see under canopy, so it does not see the main force in its base areas or most of the Trail. It cannot reliably see men on foot moving slowly or in small groups. It cannot tell a VC sampan from a farmer's, or a supply truck from a civilian lorry; its tracks are movement, not identity, and firing on them under the historical rules will kill civilians. It has no satellite links in 1964: control and data are limited to about 250 nm of a ground terminal⁹⁹, and Hanoi and Haiphong are out of reach. It depends on a reading of "granted" for navigation and landing without GPS, and on a single ground segment at Bien Hoa. It is vulnerable to the SA-2 and, unlike the U-2, has no pilot to avoid one. Four aircraft sustain one continuous orbit, not two. It does not touch the VCI. It does not reduce tonnage arriving in the South; it shows where tonnage moves in the open, and only a strike reduces it.

### D15 · What it denies the enemy
The cover of night and weather for anything that moves in the open: trucks on roads, sampans on canals and rivers, trawlers at sea, and columns crossing open ground. It forces the enemy to move under canopy or in small groups by day and in rain, which slows him and costs him. It denies the seaborne infiltration route its anonymity among coastal traffic.

### D16 · Honest verdict
A persistent, all-weather, day-and-night watch on everything that moves in the open within about 250 nm of Bien Hoa or Da Nang⁹⁹ — the sea approaches, the Delta's waterways, the roads of the Laotian panhandle and the North's southern provinces. Its strongest use is at sea, where a steel-hulled trawler is a large, slow, isolated target among junks, and the coast is otherwise unwatched; its second is the Trail's open stretches once the enemy has trucks on it from 1965. Under canopy it is blind, against the infrastructure useless, and in the Delta and the populated coastal plain it is dangerous, because it shows movement without identity. It does not find the main force where the main force lives. It shows where it moves, which is less, and it is worth what can be put on a track inside the hour.

**Key figures — P003b**

- Quantity: 4 RQ-4B Block 40⁷, almost half the 2026 USAF fleet of 9 
- Sensor: MP-RTIP AESA radar, simultaneous GMTI and SAR, maritime modes⁷¹; no camera, no SIGINT 
- Ceiling 60,000 ft; speed about 357 mph; endurance 32+ hours, 24 hours on station at 1,200 miles²
- Orbit: one continuous 24-hour orbit sustained, a second for about a third of the time¹⁰⁰
- Coverage: roughly 20,000–40,000 sq km watched for moving targets per orbit; vehicles and boats tracked to about 100–150 nm¹⁰¹
- Links: line of sight only, about 200–250 nm from a terminal¹⁰²; terminals at Bien Hoa and Da Nang
- Navigation: inertial with radar map-matching and a local positioning array at the home runway¹⁰³
- Personnel: about 380¹⁰⁴
- Cost: about US$18,900 per flight hour (2013); about US$130 million per aircraft (2013)³
- Threats: immune to guns and MiGs; highly vulnerable to the SA-2 from mid-1965⁵⁰


---

# Memorandum 003 — Part 2 of 3 · §2 Integration and second-order effects · §3 Changes to existing forces

*Audit — Memo 003, Part 2/3 · v1.0.3 · cards loaded: 002,001 · channels: 10/10 (floor 9) · findings numbered: 17 · employment concepts: 8 + 3 abandoned (floor 4) · counter-directional: yes · strongest positive: yes · designations: ~32 · structural deviation: none*

*COMUSMACV to CINCPAC · Saigon*

One correction governs everything that follows. The first part of this memorandum assumed that these aircraft arrive in a world without satellites, and drew the Global Hawk on a leash of about 250 nautical miles from a ground terminal at Bien Hoa or Da Nang. That assumption is withdrawn. By standing condition, the navigation and communications satellites these aircraft were built to use are present from the first day of the campaign⁷, and the record has been corrected accordingly (E01). Both aircraft therefore fly exactly as they fly in 2026: the Global Hawk navigates and lands by satellite navigation, is controlled and passes its picture by satellite as well as by line of sight, and can hold its orbit wherever its endurance and the enemy's missiles allow; the U-2S sends its take home by satellite from beyond the reach of any ground station¹⁰⁵. The condition brings the satellites, not the receivers. Nothing else in this command gains a satellite receiver or a satellite terminal by it, and every coordinate these aircraft produce must still be used by aircraft, guns and men who navigate by map, compass, LORAN and TACAN.

## §2 Part A — Integration

### Functional systems

These two provisions give this command, for the first time, an honest picture of the enemy's rear — and almost nothing about the war in the hamlets. That sentence is the frame for everything below. The U-2S builds the map: radar and camera imagery of the North's railways and ports, the Laotian panhandle's roads and passes, the base areas along the Cambodian border, and a signals take that locates the transmitters of the enemy's higher headquarters. The RQ-4B watches the traffic on the map: one continuous orbit, day and night, in any weather, over whatever stretch of open road, river, canal or sea I choose. With satellite control, the choice is no longer bounded by a terminal's horizon. The Global Hawk can sit over the Gulf of Tonkin and watch the coastal road from Vinh to Thanh Hoa as easily as it can sit over the Plain of Reeds.

**Finding the main force.** Both join it, and both strengthen the half of it that is about the enemy's movement rather than his presence. Neither makes it newly viable, because the enemy's regiments live under canopy and the radar and the camera stop at the treetops. What is new is that the enemy's movement in the open — the trawler standing in to the coast of Phu Yen, the truck column at the ford below the Mu Gia pass, the sampan convoy crossing the Plain of Reeds at two in the morning — is visible whenever it happens, rather than when an OV-1 Mohawk with its AN/APS-94 side-looking radar happens to be overhead. And what the U-2S's signals payload adds is a location, to kilometres, for every regimental and front headquarters that transmits¹⁰⁵. That is the find. It is not the fix.

**Interdiction.** Joined and strengthened, at sea most of all. The sea route has been the least watched of the enemy's lines since the North's maritime group began running steel-hulled trawlers south; the Junk Force and a few US Navy destroyers cannot sort one hundred-ton trawler from the tens of thousands of fishing craft on a coast of about 2,000 kilometres [M-TNA]. A wide-area maritime radar at 60,000 feet does exactly that sorting: a steel hull moving at a steady ten knots on a course from the Gulf of Tonkin toward the II Corps coast is a different object from a junk working its nets¹⁰⁶. On the Trail the radar is a lesser instrument, because the 1964 Trail is a footpath under trees and becomes a truck road only from 1965. Where trucks run in the open, it sees them.

**Strike.** Joined, and made more responsive only where a strike aircraft can reach a track within the hour. A U-2S target folder improves every sortie flown against a fixed target in the North¹⁰⁵. A Global Hawk track improves only the sorties already airborne or on strip alert, and in 1964 this command has few of either: the A-1H Skyraiders and T-28D fighter-bombers of the Vietnamese Air Force and the US Air Commandos, the B-57B bombers from August, and no night attack capability worth the name.

**Hamlet security, the advisory effort and the attack on the infrastructure.** Not joined in the first order. Nothing that flies at 60,000 feet tells a district chief who collects the Front's taxes in his villages, and I said so at the head of this memorandum¹⁰⁵. There is one narrow and real point of contact with hamlet security, through the police, and it is the subject of the first compound pairing below.

**Sustainment.** Small in tonnage, large in a scarce kind of man. About 600 people, most of them specialists — imagery analysts, signals linguists, radar operators, contractor engineers¹⁰⁵. The satellite condition lets the Global Hawks themselves leave South Vietnam: they need only a runway with their launch and recovery element, and Clark Air Base in the Philippines, about 900 nautical miles from Saigon, puts them out of mortar range at a cost of about six hours' transit on a thirty-hour sortie.

### Compound pairings
**Interactions with earlier provisions**

- P003b × P002a: the radar sees movement, the police establishes identity; a night track on a Delta canal goes to a police and Field Force checkpoint to be stopped and searched, not to a gun
- P003b × P002b: the merit-selected province chief is the clearance authority for fire in his province; whether a radar track is a target is his decision, and a chief kept for two years learns what his canals look like at night
- P003a × P003b: the U-2S builds the map and finds the new road, truck park or landing beach; the Global Hawk watches the traffic on it; together they turn a guessed logistics system into a measured one
- P003a × P001b, P002a: none beyond the general rule that the aircraft's products reach Vietnamese hands only sanitised, because the Joint General Staff and the police are penetrated; the anti-corruption and police reforms reduce that penetration only at the margin (INTEL.penetration ledger)

**F003-1** **In the Delta, the radar is worth having only if a policeman, not a gun, answers its tracks.** At night the canals of Long An, Dinh Tuong and Kien Hoa carry tens of thousands of sampans, and the Global Hawk sees every one that moves¹⁰⁵. It cannot tell the Front's tax courier from a rice merchant, and under the historical rules a moving boat after curfew in a free-fire area is a target. Fed to the artillery, the radar is an instrument for killing farmers at an industrial rate. Fed to the police, it is something new. The police reform has already put resource-control checkpoints on the Delta's main canals and given the Special Branch a file on every village committee⁶. A track that leaves a hamlet the census says should be asleep, moves along the Bao Dinh canal at 0200 and stops at a hamlet the Special Branch rates as a liaison point is a pattern a district operations room can act on: a Police Field Force section waits at the next lock at dawn, or the constable's night check notes who was not at home. Movement from the radar, identity from the police. The province chief chosen on merit holds the clearance for fire in his province, and it is his decision whether a track is a target or a question. A chief who will be in the province for two years learns what his canals look like at night and is less likely to clear fire on them than one who expects to be gone at the next coup. The weight of this pairing is moderate. It does not touch the main force; it makes the infrastructure's waterborne logistics in the held belt somewhat more visible, and above all it is the arrangement that keeps the most dangerous use of this radar from becoming its routine use.

**F003-2** **Together the two aircraft turn the enemy's rear from a guessed system into a measured one.** The U-2S finds the new things: the road cut through the forest west of the Ban Karai pass, the truck park at a ford, the beach in Phu Yen where a trawler's cargo was carried inland, the SA-2 site in its first week¹⁰⁵. The Global Hawk then watches the traffic on what the U-2 found. Neither does this alone: the U-2 looks once a day and goes away, and the Global Hawk sees movement without knowing what the road is for. Together they give this headquarters counts it has never had — trucks per night through a pass in the dry season, trawler arrivals per month, vehicle traffic on the road from Sihanoukville to the border base areas — and they give them as counts of the enemy's own activity, not as reports from units rated on what they report. That is the single most important thing these aircraft add to the war, and it is a thing about knowledge, not about destruction. A truck counted is not a truck destroyed, and a ton seen moving is not a ton denied.

### The find-to-fix chain against the main force

The chain is strengthened at its first link and nowhere else, and the weakness has moved to the second. A PAVN or main-force VC regiment in a base area in War Zone D, the Do Xa or the Kontum highlands lives under canopy and moves on foot. So will the regiments of the PAVN 325th Division when they begin to come south at the end of 1964. The Global Hawk does not see it there. The U-2S's signals payload locates its headquarters when the headquarters transmits, to a few kilometres, and its radar finds the clearings, the new trails and the river crossings that a base area cannot hide¹⁰⁵. What this gives me is a better answer to the question "where is the 9th VC Division's headquarters this week?" and no answer at all to "where are its battalions tonight?"

The regiment is seen when it leaves the trees. A column crossing open paddy at night, a battalion moving by sampan across the Plain of Reeds, a regiment's supply carried on the canals from the Delta to War Zone C: those the radar sees, if they move together and fast enough¹⁰⁵. That is where the chain now breaks. A track is true for perhaps twenty to forty minutes¹⁰⁷. In 1964 this command can put an airmobile company on the ground in UH-1B and CH-34 helicopters in about two to three hours from a standing start, and only in daylight. It can put an A-1H over the track in thirty to sixty minutes if one is on alert, and its pilot cannot see the target at night without flares. The find has outrun the fix by an order of magnitude. That gap — between a picture refreshed every few seconds and a force that answers in hours — is the most useful thing this memorandum says about the main-force war, and it is the reason the gain in finding the main force is one band and not two.

The deeper limit is the one the Theater Needs Assessment set: the enemy decides whether to accept battle, and these aircraft do not change that. A regiment that knows it is watched in the open stays out of the open, and its battle remains its choice. What changes is the cost of that choice, which is analysed under anticipation.

### The identify-to-neutralise chain against the VCI

Untouched, except through the police. The infrastructure does not move in columns or transmit on regimental nets. Its members stand in the village by day, and at night they move on foot along paths between hamlets a few hundred metres apart, in ones and twos, among farmers doing the same thing⁶. The Global Hawk cannot see a man walking slowly in a group of two¹⁰⁵. The U-2S's signals payload cannot hear a courier. The only contact these aircraft make with the infrastructure is the waterborne pattern described in F003-1, and there the identification is entirely the police's work. I say this plainly because a radar picture of the Delta at night, full of moving dots, will look to visitors like a picture of the insurgency. It is a picture of the Delta.

### Dependence on SECURE

The aircraft themselves depend on hamlet security only at their bases, but their product depends on it everywhere it is used in the populated countryside. Bien Hoa, where the U-2S detachment lives, sits in a province where the Viet Cong can bring 81mm mortars within range of the ramp, and on 1 November 1964 they did. A U-2S on an open ramp is the most expensive target in the theater. This is a hamlet-security problem in the ordinary sense: whoever holds the hamlets of Bien Hoa and Bien Long districts after dark decides whether the mortar teams can reach their firing points. The Global Hawks escape it by basing at Clark; the U-2S cannot, because its pilots, its exploitation station and its signals cell belong beside MACV.

The second dependence is through the clearance for fire. In the populated Delta and the coastal plain, every radar track that is acted on is acted on inside a province whose population is the object of the war. A province that holds its hamlets at night has a police checkpoint to send to a track. A province that does not has only artillery and aircraft, and the radar then becomes a source of civilian harm. The aircraft are only as humane as the hamlet security they are used beside.

### The ADVISE cluster

The aircraft starve the advisory effort slightly and feed it in one unexpected way. They starve it because their product cannot be shared with the Joint General Staff except in sanitised form — a grid, a time, a description — because the Joint General Staff is penetrated. The Vietnamese commander therefore acts on American information whose source and quality he cannot judge, and his staff learns nothing about collection or analysis from it. Nothing transfers under Vietnamization: the aircraft leave with the Americans, and the Republic keeps only what its commanders learned about acting quickly on a report¹⁰⁵.

They feed it because information is currency. An American adviser who can tell a Vietnamese regimental commander that a VC battalion's supply boats crossed the Vam Co Dong last night at a named place has something the commander wants. In 1964 the adviser's leverage rests on his access to American fire support and helicopters; this adds a third good to trade, and it makes the adviser more welcome at the operations table. The effect is small, and it carries the risk POSTNA-6 names: every operation launched on an American cue the Vietnamese cannot verify is an operation the Republic did not plan for itself.

### Employment concepts

What follows is what these aircraft actually do on a given week. With satellite control there are more choices than Part 1 allowed, and the most important decision is where the single Global Hawk orbit sits.

#### EC003-1 · The daily deep look (U-2S)

One U-2S sortie a day from Bien Hoa, alternating among three tracks. *North:* out over the Gulf of Tonkin, a radar and camera run down the coastal railway and Route 1A from Thanh Hoa to Dong Hoi, a signals track along the Laotian border, home; about nine hours¹⁰⁵. *Panhandle:* Mu Gia, Ban Karai, Tchepone and the Route 9 valley, then south along the Cambodian border base areas from the Fishhook to the Parrot's Beak; about seven hours. *South and sea:* the II Corps coast to 100 miles out, the Kontum and Pleiku border, the Delta's base areas in the U Minh and the Plain of Reeds; about seven hours.

The engagement sequence is collection, not attack. The radar's take goes by line of sight to the ground station at Bien Hoa when within about 220 miles and by satellite from beyond it¹⁰⁵¹⁰⁸; the station produces a first-phase readout within about an hour of collection and a formatted report to MACV J-2 and the 2nd Air Division the same evening¹⁰⁹. The second aircraft is in maintenance or on alert for a short-notice task. Recovery is at Bien Hoa, with Da Nang as alternate and Takhli as the northern alternate while the Thai government allows it¹⁰⁵.

Cycle: about one sortie a day, about 25 a month, each covering on the order of 100,000 square nautical miles of radar search¹⁰⁵. Minimum viable package: one aircraft, one pilot, a mobile officer in the chase car, and the exploitation station. Without the satellite link, the northern track's product arrives after landing rather than during the flight; with it, a target folder for a strike in the North can be in Saigon and Honolulu before the U-2 is back over Bien Hoa.

#### EC003-2 · Listening to the fronts (U-2S signals)

The U-2S's signals payload flown on long tracks along the borders, to locate the transmitters of the enemy's higher headquarters: COSVN's stations near the Cambodian border north of Tay Ninh, the Military Region 5 headquarters in the mountains of Quang Ngai and Quang Tin, the trail stations of the North's logistics command in the panhandle, and the B-3 Front's stations in the western highlands. The track is flown at the time the enemy's nets are known to come up, which the Army's 3rd Radio Research Unit at Tan Son Nhut has studied since 1961.

The engagement sequence: the payload fixes a transmitter to kilometres¹⁰⁵; the fix goes to MACV J-2 and to the radio research units, which task their own ground direction-finding and, from 1966, the EC-47 airborne direction-finding aircraft for a tighter fix; the fix goes into the target list for Arc Light or a Special Forces reconnaissance team, or into the order-of-battle file. Cycle: two to three of the daily sorties a week carry a dedicated signals track¹¹⁰. What it degrades to without the radio research units: fixes that nobody refines and therefore nobody can strike, because a few kilometres of jungle is not a target.

#### EC003-3 · The coastal picket (RQ-4B)

The Global Hawk's first orbit, from January 1964 until the panhandle orbit takes priority in the dry seasons from 1965. Station off the II Corps coast, between Qui Nhon and Cam Ranh, out to about 100 nautical miles at sea; the radar's maritime mode tracks every steel-hulled contact moving south along the coast or turning in toward it¹⁰⁵. Launch from Clark, about three hours' transit each way, about twenty-four hours on station, recovery at Clark.

The engagement sequence: a contact that behaves like a trawler — steel hull, steady course, a track from the north that does not fish — is flagged by the exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut, passed by voice and teletype to the Navy and the Vietnamese Navy's coastal command, and handed to whatever can reach it: in 1964 a Vietnamese Navy patrol craft or a Junk Force division, and the US Navy destroyers of the Seventh Fleet when they are on the coast; from March 1965 the Market Time patrol of destroyer escorts, Coast Guard 82-foot cutters and from late 1965 the PCF Swift boats. The Global Hawk holds the track for hours, so the interceptor need not find the trawler, only reach it.

Cycle: one orbit continuous; the second, when available, a third of the time¹⁰⁵. Minimum viable package: one aircraft on station, the exploitation cell, and one surface vessel within about six hours' steaming of the likely landing beach. Without a surface vessel the picket is a log of what landed where.

#### EC003-4 · Out of mortar range: Clark basing and satellite control (RQ-4B)

Not an engagement but the decision that makes the others sustainable, and only possible because of the satellite condition. The launch and recovery element and the four aircraft sit at Clark Air Base, about 900 nautical miles from Saigon; the mission control element and the exploitation cell sit at Tan Son Nhut beside MACV J-2, controlling the aircraft by satellite¹⁰⁵¹¹¹. Bien Hoa and Da Nang are divert fields, with a small launch and recovery kit at Bien Hoa for a damaged aircraft that cannot reach Clark¹¹².

What this buys: the Global Hawks are out of reach of every mortar in Vietnam, on a base with mature maintenance and no shortage of ramp space; about 140 maintainers and contractor technicians live outside the Republic. What it costs: about six hours of each thirty-hour sortie in transit, so each sortie gives about twenty-four hours on station rather than twenty-eight; the single continuous orbit still holds, with a thinner margin for the second¹¹³. A satellite outage would leave the aircraft flying its pre-programmed route home¹⁰⁵, which over water is no danger.

#### EC003-5 · The panhandle watch (RQ-4B)

From the dry season of late 1965, when trucks begin to carry a meaningful share of the Trail's load, the continuous orbit moves to the Laotian panhandle in the dry months (roughly November to April) and back to the coast in the wet. Station over the Mekong side of the panhandle, standing off from the North; the radar watches the roads from the Mu Gia and Ban Karai passes south to Tchepone and the Route 9 valley¹⁰⁵.

The engagement sequence: a column moving through a ford or a cleared stretch at night is tracked; the track goes to the airborne command post and from 1966 to the MSQ-77 radar bombing controllers of Combat Skyspot, and to any armed reconnaissance flight of F-100D, F-105D or A-1E aircraft in the area; strikes in Laos are flown under the rules the American Ambassador in Vientiane approves. The time from track to ordnance is the time it takes an aircraft already airborne to reach the point and find it in the dark, which in 1965–66 is thirty to sixty minutes and often never¹¹⁴. Cycle: one orbit, twenty-four hours a day, through the dry season; the U-2S panhandle track twice a week to find new roads. Minimum viable package: the orbit, an airborne command post, and two strike aircraft on station or on alert at Da Nang or in Thailand.

#### EC003-6 · The canal watch with the police (RQ-4B with P002a and P002b)

In the wet months, and whenever the second orbit is available, a Global Hawk over the upper Delta between the Vam Co Dong and the Bassac. The exploitation cell passes not tracks but patterns: the canal that carries traffic every night after curfew between two named hamlets, the sampan convoy that crosses into the Plain of Reeds on the nights after the harvest¹¹⁵. The patterns go, sanitised, to the province chief's operations room and the Special Branch, which compare them with the census and the village files; the answer is a checkpoint at a lock at dawn, a night check, or a Field Force ambush on a canal bank.

Cycle: one product a night per province served, two to four provinces per orbit¹¹⁶. Minimum viable package: the orbit, a district operations room with a Special Branch officer, and a Field Force section with boats. Without the police, this degrades to EC003-9, which is the reason I forbid it.

#### EC003-7 · The Gulf watch (RQ-4B)

A standoff orbit over the Gulf of Tonkin, outside the range of the North's SA-2s once they arrive, about 40–60 nautical miles off the coast¹¹⁷. From there the radar watches the coastal road and railway from Vinh to Thanh Hoa, the ferries at Quang Khe and the river mouths, and the traffic out of Haiphong toward the south¹¹⁸. Before the SA-2, it can fly closer; the MiG-17 cannot reach it at any time¹⁰⁵.

The engagement sequence: tracks of truck convoys and coastal shipping go to the Seventh Fleet's carrier task force on Yankee Station and to the Air Force's armed reconnaissance in the southern route packages, whose pilots then hunt what is moving rather than where traffic used to be¹¹⁹. It also watches the sea around any US Navy ship working in the Gulf — which, in August 1964, makes it the only sensor in the theater able to say whether patrol boats are present on a dark night. Cycle: the Gulf watch shares the single continuous orbit with the coast and the panhandle; in practice a third of the time in 1964, more during Rolling Thunder's campaigns against the southern route packages¹²⁰.

#### EC003-8 · The damage assessment run (U-2S)

From March 1965, a U-2S radar and camera pass over every major Rolling Thunder target within a day of the strike, by radar when the North's cloud hides it from the camera¹⁰⁵. The product goes to CINCPAC and the Joint Chiefs: what was hit, what was not, and what has been repaired. Cycle: two to three of the weekly northern tracks¹²¹. It degrades, without the U-2S, to the RF-101C and RF-8A aircraft that photographed targets at low level through the guns, losing aircraft to do it.

#### EC003-9 · Night fire on Delta tracks (abandoned)

Tried in the first weeks of 1964 by a corps artillery adviser who saw the picture and asked the obvious question: why not fire on the boats that move after curfew in the free-fire areas? Three nights of harassment fire on tracks in Dinh Tuong produced a hospital list in My Tho and no evidence that a single VC was among it¹²². Abandoned by my order: no radar track in a populated province is fired on unless the province chief clears it and a second source confirms it. The constraint it names is the one the dossier stated and the employment proved: the radar sees movement, not identity, and in the Delta most movement is innocent¹⁰⁵.

#### EC003-10 · Hunting the regiment in the open (abandoned)

Tried in 1964 in III Corps: keep the Global Hawk over War Zone D and the approaches to Binh Gia and hold an airmobile reaction force for the moment a main-force column was tracked crossing open ground. The radar produced plenty of tracks — farmers, woodcutters, ox-carts, ARVN patrols — and almost never a column, because the main force moved at night in files on foot under the trees and crossed open ground in small groups faster than the radar's slow-mover filter could separate them from clutter¹⁰⁵. The reaction force sat idle and the orbit was taken from the coast to do it. Abandoned by mid-1964. The constraint it names is the slow-mover problem of moving-target radar against dismounted infantry, and behind it the deeper one: the enemy chooses when to be in the open.

#### EC003-11 · Overland orbit of the North (abandoned)

Flown in the months before the SA-2 sites: a Global Hawk over the North's panhandle above Route 15 and the Mu Gia approaches, with the whole of the southern route packages in radar view. Abandoned when the first SA-2 site was photographed near Hanoi in April 1965 and finally once sites appeared south of the Red River delta. A slow, unmanoeuvrable aircraft at 60,000 feet is the missile's ideal target¹⁰⁵, and a quarter of the force is not worth a month of overland coverage when the Gulf watch sees most of the same roads from the sea. What it names is the same thing the U-2 learned over Sverdlovsk: altitude is protection only until the other side has the missile.

## §2 Part B — Channels

### Displacement

**F003-3** **The sea route does not close; it moves to Sihanoukville, a year early.** The North's maritime group ran steel-hulled trawlers of about 100 tons to the II Corps and Delta coasts from the early 1960s, and on 16 February 1965 one was caught unloading in Vung Ro Bay by an Army helicopter pilot who happened to see it. Under a continuous coastal orbit the trawlers are seen every time, and a trawler that is tracked for six hours is intercepted or turns back¹²³. Hanoi's answer is the one it made historically after Market Time: ship to Sihanoukville under the Chinese and East European flags that Sihanouk's neutrality protects, and truck the cargo to the border base areas through Cambodian contractors. Here the shift comes in 1964–65 rather than 1966¹²⁴. The capability is displaced, not denied: across a border, into a neutral port, onto roads the radar can watch from South Vietnamese airspace and no one may strike.
