What the command loses by pushing it there is the chance to seize it. A trawler intercepted off Phu Yen is a cargo captured and a crew interrogated; a freighter at Sihanoukville is a manifest we can read about and do nothing about. The weight is major, because the sea was the route that fed the coastal provinces and the Delta most directly, and because the displacement makes the Cambodian sanctuary the enemy's principal external line in the South two to three years earlier than it was.

**F003-4** **On the Trail and in the South, the enemy moves under the trees, in the rain and in the orbit's gaps.** Where the radar watches, the enemy stops showing himself in the open. Truck movement on the panhandle's roads concentrates under trellised canopy, at fords crossed in rain, and in the hours when the orbit is over the coast or the Gulf¹⁰⁵; the Delta's main-force supply moves from large sampan convoys on the main canals to small boats on the minor ones. He accepts slower movement and more porters to protect his trucks, as he later did against the Igloo White sensors and their EC-121R relay aircraft¹²⁵. What our intelligence loses by pushing him there is the picture itself. A truck column crossing an open ford at Ban Phanop told us how much was moving; the same tonnage moved under canopy in porters' loads tells us nothing until it arrives. The weight is moderate: the enemy pays in time, not in tonnage, and what he moves under the trees he would largely have moved there anyway in 1964–65.

### Bottleneck migration

**F003-5** **The binding constraint moves from finding the enemy in the open to acting before he leaves it.** Before these provisions, the binding constraint on every line of effort against the enemy's movement was that we did not see it: the coast was unwatched, the Trail was photographed when weather allowed, and the Delta's waterways at night were dark [M-TNA]. After them, we see it, and the constraint migrates to four things the provisions do not supply. *Response time:* a track lasts minutes to an hour; in 1964 nothing reaches it that fast, and at sea a surface vessel needs hours. *Night attack:* the period's strike aircraft hit what they can see, and at night they see what a flare shows them. *Clearance:* a strike in Laos needs Vientiane's rules, a strike in the South needs the province chief, a strike in Cambodia is forbidden under the present constraints. *Coordinates:* the aircraft produce satellite-accurate positions, and the force that must use them navigates by map, LORAN and TACAN; the period's 1:50,000 maps are drawn on an older datum and can differ from satellite positions by hundreds of metres¹²⁶. Of the four, the first two bind hardest. A new constraint has a further property: it is a demand. A headquarters that can see trucks at night will want aircraft that can kill them at night, and the AC-47 and, later, the AC-130 gunships will be asked for sooner and harder than history asked¹²⁷. The weight is major. Naming it is the most useful thing this memorandum does for the main-force war.

On the sea, the bottleneck migrates from detection to interception and then to jurisdiction: once the trawler is seen and the Market Time patrols can reach it, the remaining constraint is Sihanoukville, which is a political constraint. Over the North, it migrates from target intelligence to target approval: the U-2S's folders are better than anything Rolling Thunder had, and the White House still chooses the targets.

### Own-force opportunity cost

**F003-6** **The scarce thing these aircraft consume is the theater's small supply of imagery analysts and Vietnamese-speaking signals men.** About 600 spaces in all¹⁰⁵, and the number is not the cost. The cost is that the exploitation cells need imagery analysts who understand radar, and signals analysts and linguists who can work the enemy's traffic, and in 1964 the US armed forces have few of either who know Vietnam¹²⁸. Some of them are taken from the radio research units and the photo interpreters who would otherwise serve the corps and the Special Forces¹²⁹. The provisions bring their own 2026 analysts, but they need Vietnamese linguists and order-of-battle men to make sense of what they see, and those come from the 1964 pool. The second cost is in strike sorties. Every armed reconnaissance flight sent to a track is a flight not sent to the close air support of a post under attack at night, which is where the Theater Needs Assessment said air power in the South mattered most. In 1964 there are not enough strike aircraft to do both, and the picture will pull them toward the tracks¹³⁰. The weight is moderate: no lives are spent, but a scarce kind of attention and a scarce kind of man are, and some of them are taken from the half of the war the aircraft cannot see.

### Counterfactual erasure

**F003-7** **The surprises the enemy's rear used to spring stop happening.** Three historical surprises no longer happen as they did. The trawler at Vung Ro Bay in February 1965, found by luck after an unknown number of earlier landings, is found by design, and the 1964 landings on the II Corps coast are tracked. The first SA-2 site, photographed near Hanoi on 5 April 1965 and then left unstruck until the first American aircraft was lost to a missile on 24 July, is found by its radar emissions and its construction in its first weeks, and its fellows as they are built; whether they are struck before they are ready is Washington's decision, not this command's, and historically Washington declined. The rate at which trucks replaced porters on the Trail in 1965 is measured as it happens rather than reconstructed from prisoners a year later. What becomes of the attention those surprises consumed is mostly good: an order of battle argued from evidence, and a Market Time that starts with a picture of what it is looking for. The weight is moderate. The surprises still happen; they happen visibly.

**F003-8** **The phantom attack of 4 August 1964 may not survive the radar.** On the night of 4 August 1964, two US destroyers in the Gulf of Tonkin, Maddox and Turner Joy, reported themselves under torpedo attack in bad weather; the second attack almost certainly never happened, and the reports of it carried the retaliatory strikes of 5 August and the Gulf of Tonkin Resolution of 7 August. After the real attack on Maddox on 2 August, the Gulf watch is where I would put the orbit, and a maritime radar at 60,000 feet is the instrument best placed to see whether patrol boats were there¹³¹. My judgment is that the record of that night would show no boats with fair confidence — the sea state and the thunderstorms cost some sensitivity against small craft, but P-4 torpedo boats making attack runs at speed are good radar targets¹³². What follows is not mine to decide. The 2 August attack was real, the President wanted a resolution, and the doubts that existed historically were overridden within hours. A radar record raises the price of overriding them, and puts in the files a fact that, when it surfaces, will be read as proof that the government knew. I state this at its true weight: possibly war-shaping, and uncertain in every step after the radar.

### Perverse effect

**F003-9** **A radar that shows every moving boat makes it easier to kill farmers.** This is the danger I named in the first part¹⁰⁵, and in the second I find it is worse than a risk of error: it is an incentive. A free-fire area at night has always been defined by the absence of anyone to see what moved in it. Now someone sees everything that moves, and the commander who has a picture of fifty moving dots and an artillery battery will be asked every night why he does not fire. EC003-9 is abandoned by order, and the order will be tested by every corps and division commander who sees the picture. Where it fails, the result is civilian harm in exactly the provinces the land and police programmes are trying to win, and a refugee flow from the canals to the towns (POP.civilian_harm). The weight is moderate, and it is the one finding in this section whose control depends entirely on discipline.

**F003-10** **Better pictures make Washington's control of the air war tighter, not looser.** The U-2S's folders go to Honolulu and Washington within hours¹³³. The Rolling Thunder target lists were chosen at the President's Tuesday lunches from photographs and summaries; better photographs, faster, make that system work better on its own terms. The perverse result is that the satellite link brings Washington closer to the target, not the theater commander, and that the air war's graduated pressure — a theory of signals to Hanoi's leaders — is supported by a better picture of the targets that its theory chose¹³⁴. The weight is minor to moderate. It does not make the air war worse. It makes its central flaw more efficient.

### Anticipation

**F003-11** **The enemy stops moving in the open, and the counts these aircraft give will fall while nothing has changed.** The aircraft's existence prevents much of what would demonstrate it. Once the enemy knows that a column in the open at night is seen — and he will learn it within months — main-force movement in the open falls, sea infiltration moves to Sihanoukville, and trucks on the panhandle move in rain and under trees¹⁰⁵. The track counts rise in the first months of each orbit and then fall. A headquarters that reads the fall as success will report that infiltration is down when it has only moved where the radar cannot see. A headquarters that reads the falling truck kills on the Trail as failure will ask for more strike sorties when the enemy has simply re-routed. Both are A13 errors. The real effect is the one the counts cannot show: an enemy who moves only under trees and at the radar's edges moves more slowly, and a regiment that must avoid open ground cannot concentrate as fast or as far. That is a genuine improvement in the war that no metric records, and I state it at its weight: moderate in 1964, larger when the main force is larger and more dependent on trucks and boats. The anticipation also applies at sea with full force: a trawler route that is abandoned produces no interceptions, and the success there is invisible except as cargo that never lands on the II Corps coast.

### Enemy detection and learning

**F003-12** **Hanoi reads the U-2 at once, the Global Hawk within months, and draws one wrong conclusion that helps us.** The North's P-12 early-warning radars track the U-2S from its first sortie, and its leaders know what a U-2 is; China has shot down Nationalist U-2s since 1962. They conclude correctly that the Americans can photograph and map everything above the canopy, and they ask Moscow for missiles, which they would have done anyway after the bombing began. The Global Hawk is slower to understand: a large aircraft at 60,000 feet that circles for a day at a time over the sea or the panhandle is seen on radar, but what it does is learned from experience — a trawler intercepted without warning off Binh Dinh, a truck column bombed at night at a ford, a sampan convoy met at a lock¹³⁵. By the end of 1964 the Party's logistics cadres conclude, correctly, that movement in the open is seen at night and in rain when the aircraft is up. The doctrine propagates through the logistics command and Military Region 5 within one season, and to COSVN's units in the Delta within a year¹³⁶. They also conclude wrongly that the Americans hear their radio traffic better than they do, and tighten radio discipline beyond what the U-2S's signals payload actually requires; that slows their own command, and it is the one place their learning helps us. What they get right and hurts us: that the radar is a single orbit, that its absence is visible on their own radar, and that the hours when it is over the Gulf are the hours to move on the Trail.

### Enemy adaptation cost

**F003-13** **Most of the enemy's counters are cheap; the sea is the exception.** Moving under the trees and in rain costs him time and porters, not tonnage; the Trail's capacity is elastic with labour, and its requirement in 1964–65 is small. The cost to us is about 600 specialists and two expensive fleets; the cost to him is a slower Trail. That exchange is in his favour. At sea it is different. The trawler route was cheap, direct and delivered to the coast of the provinces where the main force fought; Sihanoukville is longer, depends on a neutral prince's tolerance and on Cambodian contractors' bribes, and can be closed by a change of government in Phnom Penh. The enemy pays for that route in dependence: the Cambodian supply line is exactly as secure as Sihanouk's neutrality. Radio discipline costs him in command and control at regiment and above, which is the one adaptation that costs him something he values. Overall, the provisions make the enemy busy more than they make him poor, and that judgment should be read beside the strongest positive effect, which is about knowledge, not cost.

### Cross-boundary feedback

**F003-14** **The loop out of theater runs through losses, the Gulf of Tonkin and honest damage reports.** Three loops matter. *A lost U-2* is a public event, with the Powers trial in every Congressman's memory¹⁰⁵; over the North after the SA-2, a loss produces a prisoner and a show trial, and a political cost far beyond the aircraft. The Global Hawk's loss produces no prisoner, and its political cost is a headline. *The Gulf of Tonkin*: if the radar record of 4 August shortens or weakens the resolution, the American war begins on a narrower legal and political base, and the later discovery that the 4 August attack did not happen — which historically fed the credibility gap from 1968 — either happens earlier or becomes impossible to hide. Either way the clock is affected at its root, in which direction Part 3 must decide. *Honest damage assessment*: the U-2S's daily picture of Rolling Thunder targets tells Washington the truth about the bombing's effects sooner, which reduces the gap between what this command reports and what the press later learns, and also tells Washington sooner that the North repairs faster than it bombs. That is bad news delivered early, which is the kind that buys time. The weight is moderate overall, potentially major through the Gulf of Tonkin.

### Open channel

**F003-15** **The aircraft watch China too, and an honest picture of the Chinese in the North lowers the risk of misjudging them.** From June 1965 China sent engineering, railway and anti-aircraft troops into the North, eventually in large numbers. A U-2S signals and radar track along the northern border, and the Gulf watch near Hainan, see their deployments, their camps and their railway work¹³⁷. That is a channel the nine above do not name: these aircraft give Washington evidence of what China is and is not doing, and so reduce the risk of the misjudgment that every American planner feared after Korea. It also opens a risk: an aircraft lost near Hainan or the Chinese border is an incident with Peking, and I do not fly the Global Hawk within radar sight of Hainan's fighters without CINCPAC's approval. The weight is moderate.

## §2 Part C — Counter-directional finding and strongest positive effect

**F003-16 · Counter-directional** **The war we can see will draw the command away from the war we cannot.** These aircraft show the enemy's rear in detail no headquarters has had: the trucks, the trawlers, the roads, the transmitters. They show nothing of the infrastructure in the hamlets, which the Theater Needs Assessment and the first two memoranda placed at the centre of the war. A commander with a live picture of the Trail and the coast will spend his attention, his strike sorties and his staff's best officers on what the picture shows, and Washington, which sees the same picture, will judge him on it. The risk is that the main-force and interdiction war — the half A6 says must never be conflated with the other — becomes still more the war that this command fights, because it is the war it can see. I state the weight at moderate: it does not undo the land, police and chief reforms, but it competes with them for the command's attention in the year they most need it, and the counts these aircraft produce are exactly the kind of numbers that drive a headquarters' priorities.

**F003-17 · Strongest positive** **For the first time, this command can measure the enemy's external supply rather than guess it, and the sea route that fed the coastal provinces is shut.** Two effects, both large. The first is measurement. Historically, MACV and the CIA argued for years over how much reached the South through Sihanoukville, and the argument was settled only by captured records after Lon Nol's coup in 1970. Here the coastal picket, the panhandle watch and a radar standing off the Cambodian border count trawlers, trucks and the road traffic from Sihanoukville as the enemy generates them. That is the kind of metric A13 asks for and the theater has never had: a measure of the enemy's activity, not of our units' claims. It does not reduce the tonnage, but it makes every argument about interdiction, sanctuary and the cost of the war honest from 1964 rather than from 1970, and it makes the case for or against relief of the Cambodian constraint on evidence. The second is the sea. The steel trawlers that historically delivered arms directly to the II Corps and Delta coasts are seen every time, and the route is abandoned for Sihanoukville within about a year. The weapons that came by sea in 1964 — the AK-47 rifles, the RPG-2 launchers and the ammunition that re-armed the main force before the 1964–65 offensive — reach the South more slowly and by a longer road¹³⁸. I put the weight at major, not transformative: the enemy's requirement is small and his other routes are elastic. If F003-8 plays out as it might, the strongest effect of this memorandum will be on the Gulf of Tonkin, and it will be political, not military.

## §2 Findings register
| ID | Channel | System | Summary | Weight |
|---|---|---|---|---|

| F003-10 | Perverse effect | STRIKE | Faster, better pictures tighten Washington's control of target selection and make graduated pressure more efficient | minor |

**Employment concepts**

EC003-1 the daily deep look (U-2S) · EC003-2 listening to the fronts (U-2S signals) · EC003-3 the coastal picket (RQ-4B) · EC003-4 Clark basing and satellite control (RQ-4B) · EC003-5 the panhandle watch (RQ-4B) · EC003-6 the canal watch with the police (RQ-4B with P002a, P002b) · EC003-7 the Gulf watch (RQ-4B) · EC003-8 the damage assessment run (U-2S). Abandoned: EC003-9 night fire on Delta tracks (movement without identity kills farmers) · EC003-10 hunting the regiment in the open (slow movers under canopy; the enemy chooses when to be seen) · EC003-11 overland orbit of the North (the SA-2)

## §3 Changes to existing forces

### The advisory effort and MACV staff

MACV J-2 changes most. It acquires an exploitation cell of about 250 people beside the headquarters at Tan Son Nhut, a live radar picture, and a daily strategic take¹⁰⁵, and it must build in months what it historically built over years: a combined intelligence centre able to fuse the aircraft's product with the radio research units' signals, prisoner interrogation and captured documents. The order-of-battle branch, historically a few officers arguing from prisoners and documents, now argues from counts. The advisers in the field change modestly: a province or regimental adviser receives sanitised reports — a grid, a time, a description — and must pass them to his counterpart without revealing their source (§2 Part A, the ADVISE cluster).

### US conventional forces

Unchanged in 1964, when there are none in combat. From 1965 the US divisions receive the same sanitised cues as ARVN and, more usefully, the U-2S's radar maps of their areas of operations, which show trails, clearings and river crossings that the period's maps do not¹⁰⁵. The 1st Cavalry Division's reconnaissance squadron and the brigades' long-range patrols get better places to look. The divisions' search-and-destroy operations do not change in kind: the main force still chooses whether to fight.

### US Special Forces and CIDG

The border camps and the reconnaissance projects gain most among the ground forces. A Special Forces reconnaissance team inserted on a U-2S signals fix or a radar map of new trails starts from a better guess¹⁰⁵; the team still has to find the enemy on foot. The CIDG camps along the Cambodian and Laotian borders get warning of movement in the open near them at night, from the panhandle watch and the border track, which is warning they have historically lacked¹³⁹.

### ARVN divisions and the JGS

The Joint General Staff receives nothing it can verify and much it can use: weekly sanitised summaries of movement near the corps' borders, and specific cues through the advisory chain. Its penetration by the enemy limits what it can be told¹⁰⁵. The divisions do not change: they cannot act on a track faster than they could act on a rumour, and the find-to-fix gap applies to them with greater force than to US units.

### RF/PF and territorial security

Unchanged in substance. The Regional and Popular Forces in the Delta gain an occasional dawn ambush on a canal from the canal watch, working with the police. Their real business — sleeping in the hamlets and answering a call at night — is untouched by any aircraft.

### Rolling Thunder

Changed in its intelligence, not its politics. From March 1965 its targets are selected from U-2S imagery and its damage assessed by the daily deep look, its route planning starts from an electronic order of battle of the North's radars and SA-2 sites¹⁰⁵, and the armed reconnaissance missions in the southern route packages hunt traffic the Gulf watch shows them. Fewer sorties are wasted on targets already down, and fewer aircraft are lost learning where the guns are. The targets are still chosen in Washington.

### Trail interdiction

Barrel Roll from December 1964 and Steel Tiger from April 1965 fly against a measured Trail: new roads found by the U-2S's radar change detection, trucks in the open tracked by the panhandle watch, from the dry season of 1965–66. Strikes cued by a live track kill more trucks per sortie where aircraft are already airborne¹⁴⁰. They do not change the judgment that interdiction is measured by tonnage arriving below requirement, and the enemy's answer, under the trees and in the rain, is cheap.

### Arc Light

From June 1965 the B-52 strikes in the South gain better target boxes: base areas located by signals fixes and radar maps rather than by agent reports alone¹⁰⁵. The bombing still falls on jungle where the enemy was, and the aircraft do not tell us whether he was still there. Damage assessment of a B-52 box under canopy remains guesswork; the U-2S's radar sees the craters, not the dead.

### Airmobile formations

Unchanged in tempo; changed in tasking at the margin. An airmobile reaction force held for a radar track was tried and abandoned. From 1965 the 1st Cavalry Division uses the radar maps for landing-zone selection and the movement picture for warning, not for triggering assaults.

### Riverine and coastal forces

Changed most of all. The Junk Force and the Vietnamese Navy's coastal patrols in 1964, and Market Time from March 1965, operate against a tracked sea: the coastal picket tells them where a trawler is and where it is going, so the patrol's task becomes interception rather than search¹⁴¹. Market Time's destroyer escorts, Coast Guard cutters and Swift boats are fewer for the same effect, or the same number for a much denser screen. The Vietnamese Navy's river assault groups in their LCM-6 craft, and Game Warden on the Delta's rivers from late 1965, receive the canal watch's patterns through the police and the Vietnamese Navy's river groups. The trawler route closes.

### Phoenix / Phung Hoang

Unchanged. The aircraft give the attack on the infrastructure nothing but the occasional waterborne pattern in the Delta, which goes to the Special Branch through EC003-6. The infrastructure's half of the war is the police's.

### Third-country forces

The Australian task force in Phuoc Tuy from 1966 and the Korean divisions on the II Corps coast receive sanitised cues through MACV like ARVN. The Korean divisions' coastal provinces benefit indirectly from the closed trawler route.

### Prior provisions

- **P002a** (National Police Reform): its resource-control checkpoints on the Delta canals and its Special Branch gain a source of movement patterns at night; its district operations rooms are the means by which a radar track becomes an arrest or nothing, rather than a fire mission.
- **P002b** (merit-selected province and district chiefs): the province chief's clearance of fire becomes a nightly decision about radar tracks; a chief who stays two years learns to refuse fire on unidentified movement.
- Unchanged: P001a (the land programme; its survey photography is not affected by these aircraft, and its satellite-navigation correction is in E02), P001b (the anti-corruption programme).

# Memorandum 003 — Part 3 of 3 · §4 The course of the war · §5 Delta ledger · §6 Changed / not changed

*Audit — Memo 003, Part 3/3 · v1.0.3 · phases revised: 5 (P1, P2, P3, P4, P5), nulled: 1 · events created or changed: 18 (P1 5, P2 4, P3 3, P4 3, P5 3; floor 3/phase) · new operations: 2 · chains reaching link 3: 4 (floor 3) · phases carrying a §2 finding: 5/5 · strongest positive grounded: yes · clock stated: 3 phase(s) · enemy initiative: 1 · free play: 0 · ripple flags: 1 (P5→P6) · end state: unchanged (rung a) · structural deviation: none*

## §4.0 The course of the war

The war these aircraft fly over is the war that ends on 30 April 1975 with the fall of Saigon, and they do not move that date by a day. What they change is what this command, and Washington, know about the enemy while the war is being lost, and when they know it. That is less than a battle won and more than it sounds, because this was a war in which the gap between what was reported and what was true became, by 1968, the thing that decided it.

On the first day of 1964 the Republic has a land decree, a rural police and a list of examined province chiefs, all new, and Saigon has a junta two months old that will not survive the month. It also has, from the same morning, the best strategic reconnaissance in the world. Two U-2S fly from a revetment on the north side of Bien Hoa, one sortie a day, alternating between the North's coast, the Laotian panhandle and the South's border base areas, with a radar that sees through the monsoon and a signals payload that hears every regimental transmitter that comes up. Four Global Hawks fly from Clark, out of mortar range, and hold one radar orbit over whatever stretch of sea or open ground I choose, day and night, in any weather. Neither sees through the canopy and neither sees a man in a hamlet. I said so in the first part of this memorandum, and the war bears it out in every phase.

The first place the aircraft bite is the sea. The North's maritime group had been running steel trawlers of about a hundred tons down the coast since 1962, landing arms on the beaches of Phu Yen and Khanh Hoa and in the mangrove creeks of Ca Mau, and nobody was watching for them, because on a coast of two thousand kilometres and tens of thousands of junks nobody could. On the night of 18 February 1964 the Global Hawk watched one come down from the Gulf of Tonkin, turn in toward Vung Ro at dusk, and run for seven hours while a Vietnamese Navy submarine chaser steamed out to meet her. Her crew blew her up off the beach. By June the picket had tracked eleven trawlers, intercepted four and turned six back, and the prisoners had told us about two years of voyages we had never known were made. That is TIDE TABLE, and it is the cleanest thing these aircraft do in the whole war: a route that was cheap, direct and invisible became expensive and visible, and the enemy gave it up.

He gave it up for Sihanoukville. By the winter of 1964–65 the Party's military commission had concluded that we saw every ship at sea, and through Chinese negotiators and Cambodian officers on commission it opened the port at Kompong Som and the roads from it to the depots behind the Fishhook and the Parrot's Beak. The first arms cargo came ashore there in June 1965, about eighteen months before it did historically. I want to state the consequence at its true weight, because it is the most important thing in Phase I that the aircraft cause: we closed a route we could intercept and opened one we could not touch. A trawler taken off Binh Dinh is a cargo captured and a crew interrogated. A freighter at Sihanoukville is a manifest we can read and do nothing about. From mid-1965 the enemy's principal line into the southern half of the country ran through a neutral prince's port, and it stayed there until the prince fell in 1970.

The second place they bite is a night in August. When the torpedo boats attacked the *Maddox* on 2 August, Admiral Sharp asked me to put the orbit over the Gulf, and I did. Two nights later the *Maddox* and the *Turner Joy* fired for two hours at contacts in a rainstorm, and Major Kessling's watch in the cell at Tan Son Nhut, looking at the same sea from 60,000 feet, saw the destroyers, a cluster of junks thirty miles west, the rain cells, and no boats. The report reached Washington before the President had ordered anything. He did not order Pierce Arrow. The 64 sorties against the torpedo-boat bases and the oil at Vinh were not flown, Lieutenant Sather was not killed and Lieutenant Alvarez was not taken prisoner that day. The resolution went to Congress anyway, because the President had carried a draft since May and the attack of the 2nd had been real, and it passed on the 10th in the historical words. I do not claim that the radar kept the United States out of the war; it did not, and nothing in this memorandum suggests that it could. I claim that the war's only legislative authority rested on an attack that happened, and that the story which historically opened the credibility gap at its root, that the government had gone to war on an attack it knew to be doubtful, does not exist in this war.

The rest of Phase I runs as history ran it, with better maps. Hanoi commits the 325th Division's regiments in the autumn of 1964, on its own schedule and under canopy where nothing we fly can follow them. A mortar team at Bien Hoa on 1 November holes a U-2S in its revetment along with the B-57s, and for seven weeks the theater has one; I built steel shelters and asked for a Regional Forces company in the hamlets of the approaches, which is the hamlet-security problem in its plainest form. The SA-2 sites around Hanoi are mapped as they are built from March 1965, and Washington declines to strike them, as it declined historically. On 24 July a missile kills Captain Fobair's F-4C all the same, from a battery that moved overnight; on the 27th the retaliation is aimed at the one occupied site rather than at an empty one ringed with guns, and loses two F-105s rather than the losses history records. The coups in Saigon, Binh Gia, Song Be, Dong Xoai and the July decision are untouched.

Phase II is the main-force war, and it is the phase where the aircraft's limits are most visible, because the main force lives where they cannot see. The 1st Cavalry Division at the Ia Drang, the Marines at Con Thien, the sweeps of Cedar Falls and Junction City: none of them is changed in its course by a radar that stops at the treetops. A regiment in War Zone C or the Chu Pong massif still chooses when to fight, and the aircraft give me a better answer to where its headquarters is this week and no answer to where its battalions are tonight. What changes is the enemy's rear, which is now counted. In the dry season of 1965–66 the panhandle watch counts the trucks through the Mu Gia and Ban Karai passes, about 20 a night in December rising to about 120 in March, and STEADY COUNT puts strike aircraft on the tracks; they reach one in six within forty minutes and kill trucks in one in twenty, and the pilots claim more than twice what the radar shows. On 12 April 1966 the B-52s strike Mu Gia for the first time, and twenty hours later the radar watches the first trucks drive through the craters. By the measure we now have, about 70 to 90 tons a day are reaching the South through the panhandle, which is more than the forces there need from the North. The bombing raises the toll on the transit and does not lower what arrives. I have known that since the Theater Needs Assessment; now I can show it.

The U-2S shows something else in the summer of 1966: drums. Thousands of them under the trees beside the roads south of Hanoi, before the President authorises the strikes on the bulk fuel farms. The strikes go in on 29 June because the President has decided, and a better photograph does not alter the theory that chose the target. Four weeks later the Gulf watch has counted the trucks on the coastal road and found no fall, and J-2 tells the Joint Chiefs on 1 August that the fuel campaign has failed, about three months before the intelligence community reached that verdict historically. McNamara's doubts harden in August instead of the autumn, and the barrier he commissions is built in 1967 with its sensor strings laid where the radar's tracks disappear under canopy. By March 1967 the count of convoys out of Kompong Speu has settled the oldest argument in the intelligence community: MACV and the CIA agree that Sihanoukville supplies most of the arms in III and IV Corps, three years before captured ledgers settled it historically. The Joint Chiefs ask to quarantine the port. The President declines. The evidence changes the argument, and no rule.

That is the shape of the aircraft's whole war, and Tet shows its darkest side. From December 1967 the radar watches the 304th Division come down Route 9 out of Laos and the trucks of Group 559 turn east toward Khe Sanh, and the U-2S fixes the headquarters of the 325C and the 304th and hears a front headquarters forming above them. It is the most complete picture of an enemy concentration this command ever held, and every element of it is true. It goes every morning to Saigon and every evening to the White House, where the President has a terrain model of Khe Sanh in the Situation Room. Meanwhile the battalions that will attack Saigon, Hue and the provincial towns move into them in twos and threes among the Tet crowds, and on the canals of Dinh Tuong the radar records the holiday traffic as three times the usual, which it always was. The picture did not create the American fixation on Khe Sanh. It deepened it, and it made the war on the border the war that Washington could see. When the blow fell on the cities it fell on a headquarters and a President who had been shown the enemy's main force every day, and it was not where the main force was. At Lang Vei the radar gave forty minutes' warning of the tanks, and the camp fell anyway, with fewer dead.

The decision of 31 March stands on its date. The bombing halt of 1 November stands, and the Gulf watch, still counting from international waters, measures exactly what Hanoi does with it: columns of forty trucks by day on Route 1A, trains running to Vinh, traffic toward Mu Gia tripled by December. Commando Hunt opens with that count in hand and keeps a second ledger beside the Air Force's claims from its first night. The claims say 7,322 trucks destroyed in the first dry season; the radar, counting trucks in at the passes and out at the southern roads, says 2,600 to 3,200 and about a sixth of the tonnage. From 1969 the two numbers travel together to Washington, and the Defense Intelligence Agency's model never reports zero trucks on a Trail that the radar shows full every night.

Phase IV is the drawdown, and the aircraft spend it measuring the enemy's recovery. The Cambodian incursion of 1970 goes in on a map five years in the making: the depots where the convoys stopped, the roads between them, the clearings they hid in. The 1st Cavalry lands on the great complex it found by chance historically on its first day, and most of the caches fall in ten days rather than through May and June. The President names COSVN as the objective on 30 April, six days after we told him its transmitters had moved west, because the speech is written in Washington. Lam Son 719 goes in against an estimate of the enemy twice the size of the one history gave its planners, and goes in anyway, and fails for the reasons it failed historically: the landing zones, the command, the absence of American advisers. The radar kills tanks on Route 9 and puts the B-52s on the roads behind the retreating columns. It does not make the ARVN's withdrawal anything but a retreat.

Phase V is the test of Vietnamization, and the aircraft pass the part of it that is theirs. The Gulf watch sees the armour massing north of the DMZ from 12 March 1972 and MACV warns I Corps on the 27th; General Lam expects the blow from the west, and the 3rd Division's regiments are changing positions when the guns open on the 30th. The cloud that grounds the fighter-bombers does not ground the radar, and the B-52s, bombing by radar through it, catch three armoured columns in the open at the Ben Hai crossings. Camp Carroll still surrenders and Captain Ripley still blows the bridge at Dong Ha. Quang Tri still falls. Linebacker chooses from target folders seven years in the making and counts its effect on the North's own roads. At An Loc the B-52 boxes of 10–12 May fall on the assembly areas the radar shows. The PAVN's losses in tanks and men are somewhat higher than history's, and the ARVN holds where the B-52s came and breaks where its commanders failed, exactly as it did.

Phase VI is the war without the Americans, and these aircraft are American. Whatever they show in 1974 and early 1975 tells Saigon and Washington what both historically already knew from their own sources: that the North was building roads, a pipeline and an army of corps, and that it would choose its moment. Knowledge was not the deficit in 1975. Aid, fuel, ammunition, a stable command and the will in Washington to send the bombers back were, and nothing that flies at 60,000 feet supplies any of them. The PAVN takes Phuoc Long in January, Ban Me Thuot in March, and Saigon on 30 April 1975.

So the line holds. Where these aircraft bend it, they bend it toward honesty: a war that begins on a true attack rather than a doubtful one, an interdiction campaign scored by what arrives rather than by what burns, a sanctuary argued from counts rather than guesses, a bombing campaign whose failures reach the Secretary of Defense in weeks rather than seasons. Every one of those effects lengthens the political clock a little, because the clock ran fastest when Americans discovered that what they had been told was false. None of them changes the main-force war under the canopy, the war in the hamlets, or the North's decision, held from 1963 to 1975, to win. The best reconnaissance in the world has made this a better-understood defeat.

## §4.P1 Phase I — The advisory war and escalation (Jan 1964 – Jul 1965)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: POP.gvn_legitimacy +1 · FIND.main_force +1 · FIND.vci +1.*

The war that COMUSMACV inherits on 1 January 1964 is an advisory war that is being lost. Two months earlier the coup of 1 November 1963 had killed Ngo Dinh Diem and his brother Nhu, and with them went the province chiefs, the Strategic Hamlet Program and most of the administrative continuity the Republic of Vietnam possessed. The Military Assistance Command, Vietnam, under General Paul D. Harkins, is a headquarters of advisers, helicopter companies and support troops; the United States has about 23,300 military personnel in the country by the end of 1964, none of them formed into combat units, and 147 die by hostile action that year¹⁴². The fighting is done by the Army of the Republic of Vietnam, the Regional and Popular Forces, and the Civilian Irregular Defense Group camps of the US Special Forces, against a Viet Cong movement that has rebuilt itself since 1959 on the political infrastructure left from the war against France and on cadres regrouped to the North in 1954 and now returning down the Trail.

Phase I runs from January 1964 to the end of July 1965. Its shape is a double collapse and a double response. In Saigon, the state comes apart: between January 1964 and June 1965 the government changes hands by coup or forced resignation repeatedly, and the generals spend more of their energy on each other than on the countryside. In the field, the Viet Cong move from the guerrilla phase to the mobile-war phase, forming regiments and then a division in the provinces around Saigon, and at Binh Gia, Song Be, Ba Gia and Dong Xoai they destroy ARVN battalions in set-piece battles. In Hanoi, the Politburo decides that the moment for decisive victory has come and commits regular PAVN regiments to the South for the first time. The American response comes in two steps: from the air, beginning with the reprisal strikes of August 1964 and February 1965 and hardening into Rolling Thunder in March 1965; and on the ground, beginning with two Marine battalions sent to guard an airfield in March 1965 and ending with the President's decision of July 1965 to commit US ground forces to the war in strength.

One thing in this phase is new. On the first day of 1964 the junta promulgates a land reform that ends rent on tenanted rice land, gives the land free to the man who works it and confirms to him whatever the Viet Minh or the Front had already given, together with an independent commission against corruption backed by an American power to suspend aid. Neither touches the main force, and the double collapse in Saigon and in the field runs much as before. What changes, slowly and only where the government holds the hamlets, is what the government's arrival means to a tenant: by the end of July 1965 about 330,000 hectares are titled, the rent stop holds in most government-held districts, and the first musters have shown the RVNAF that it is about 41,000 men smaller than its rolls¹⁴³. The reforms also give the coup-makers a new grievance and Hanoi a new reason to hurry.

Every one of the political constraints that will shape the war is drawn in this phase: the restricted zones around Hanoi and Haiphong, the bar on ground operations in Laos and Cambodia, the reliance on presidential approval for targets in the North, and the decision to fight without calling up the reserves. So is the enemy's basic answer to American firepower: the decision to close in, choose the time of battle, and keep his base areas beyond the border.

A second change runs beside the first. The same decrees of 1 January gave the Republic a rural police and a system of choosing its province and district chiefs by examination, from a ranked list, for at least two years. Khanh's coup tested both within a month and both survived it. By July 1965 about 1,100 village police posts stand in the held belt, about two-thirds of the chiefs have been appointed through the list, and the Special Branch has begun to take the Front's village committees on evidence; the Front has killed about 410 constables, almost all in posts settled ahead of night security¹⁴⁴. None of it touches the main-force war or Saigon's coups.

A third change runs from the same first day, and it is a change in what this command can see rather than in what the Republic is. Two U-2S and four Global Hawks begin flying in January 1964, and the enemy's rear stops being a matter of guesswork. The coastal picket, TIDE TABLE, sees the trawlers of the North's maritime group every time they run south and closes the sea route within a year; Hanoi moves its southern supply to Sihanoukville about eighteen months before it did historically. On the night of 4 August 1964 the radar shows no torpedo boats near the destroyers, Pierce Arrow is not flown, and the Southeast Asia Resolution passes on the real attack of 2 August alone. The SA-2 sites are mapped as they are built, and Washington still declines to strike them. None of it touches the coups in Saigon, the summer offensive or the July decision.

#### The Land-to-the-Tiller Decree and the Commission Against Corruption
*1 Jan 1964 · Saigon · new · EV001-1*
On 1 January 1964 the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh, with Prime Minister Nguyen Ngoc Tho's cabinet, promulgated two decree-laws drafted with the US Mission over the preceding weeks. The first ended the collection of rent on tenanted rice land from the coming main-season harvest, made every present tiller the provisional owner of the land he worked up to 3 hectares in the Delta and 1 hectare in the Central Lowlands, set the landlord's retention of tenanted land at zero and his compensation at 2.5 times the land's annual paddy yield, 20% in cash and the rest in eight-year bonds indexed to the paddy price, and confirmed by name every distribution the Viet Minh or the Front had made to the man now tilling the land. The second created the Commission Against Corruption under a Commissioner appointed for six years, Judge Truong Vinh Khoa of the Saigon Court of Appeal, a Special Court for Corruption, a Pay and Muster Verification Service, and a Joint US–GVN Aid Compliance Board whose American co-chair, the Deputy Chief of Mission, could suspend aid to a named command, importer or province and whose concurrence was needed to remove the Commissioner⁵.

Minh read the land decree on Radio Saigon that evening; its first sentence was the confirmation of the Front's distributions. Within a week the Vietnamese Information Service's loudspeaker teams and leaflets had carried it to the district towns of the Delta and the coastal Centre. The landowners' associations of Saigon and Can Tho protested to the council within days, and the protest was carried by officers of landed families who sat on it. Two USAF-contracted survey aircraft began photographing the rice lands from Tan Son Nhut on 13 January, tied to ground control laid by an Army topographic detachment from the old French triangulation; they finished the Delta in early April and the coastal Centre in May¹⁴³.

#### The Police and Provincial Administration decrees; the first list
*1 Jan – 27 Apr 1964 · Saigon · new · EV002-1*
On 1 January 1964, in the same session that promulgated the land and anti-corruption decrees, the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh promulgated two more decree-laws drafted with the US Mission and the aid mission's Public Safety Division. The first rebuilt the National Police around the village: a resident post of 8 to 12 constables in every village the government held, a Police Special Branch with sole charge of intelligence on the Viet Cong infrastructure, a Police Field Force to be raised at once rather than in 1966, a national identity card and family census, a pay and career statute, and magistrate review of every arrest within 72 hours, with detention without trial capped at two years and reviewed every six months. The Directorate General was given to Vo Dinh Cuong, a career inspector of the old Sûreté who had served in the police under the French and under Diem and had kept out of Nhu's political service, for a fixed term removable only for cause¹⁴⁴. The second decree created the Provincial Administration Selection Board, seven members under Judge Dinh Quang Tuyen of the Court of Cassation, and an Inspectorate of Provincial Administration, and made unlawful the appointment of any province chief, district chief or deputy for administration who was not taken from the Board's ranked list⁶.

The two decrees were read on Radio Saigon on the evening of 1 January after the land decree, and drew less notice than it did; the landowners protested the land decree, and the officers who would lose their provinces said nothing yet, because there was nothing yet to lose. The British Advisory Mission under R. G. K. Thompson, in Saigon since 1961, lent four Special Branch officers from the Federation of Malaysia to write the Special Branch's first manuals; the first 2,500 recruits a month entered the police training centres at Vung Tau and Da Lat from February. The Board's written examination was sat on 2 March at the National Institute of Administration by about 1,640 officers and civil servants, of whom about 1,000 were judged credible after the three-day assessments held through March and April¹⁴⁴.

On 27 April 1964 the Board published its first list in the official gazette: ranked candidates for 31 posts, the 6 province chiefs and 25 district chiefs whom the Inspectorate's first sweep had found worst, nine of them in Khanh's "acting" appointments of February. Khanh's government appointed 27 of the 31 from the first name on each list, 3 from the second and 1 from the third; it refused no list. By 31 December 1964 about 95 of the Republic's roughly 290 chiefs had been appointed through the list, a third of the whole, and the Board's register of candidates had become the first record the Republic had ever kept of what its officers could actually do¹⁴⁴.

#### ◆ Operation TIDE TABLE
*6 Jan – 30 Jun 1964 · The II Corps coast from Qui Nhon to Cam Ranh Bay (Binh Dinh, Phu Yen and Khanh Hoa Provinces) and the Ca Mau coast of An Xuyen Province · new · EV003-1*
The first Global Hawk orbit went up from Clark Air Base on 6 January 1964 and took station off the II Corps coast, about 60 nautical miles out between Qui Nhon and Cam Ranh Bay, with its radar's maritime mode sweeping a belt of sea about 150 nautical miles deep¹⁰⁵. The operation was named TIDE TABLE. Its quarry was the steel-hulled trawlers of about 100 tons run south from Haiphong and the Do Son peninsula by the PAVN's maritime transport group, Group 759, which became Group 125 of the PAVN Navy in the course of 1964. Its hunters were few: the Vietnamese Navy's Sea Force, with a handful of PC-461-class submarine chasers and PCE escorts out of Qui Nhon and Nha Trang, the motorised and sail junks of the Junk Force's coastal divisions, and, when the Seventh Fleet lent them, a US destroyer or two off the coast. Before January nobody had been looking for the trawlers at all; the coast is about 2,000 kilometres long and carried tens of thousands of fishing junks [M-TNA].

The first track came on the night of 18–19 February. A contact moving at a steady ten knots on a course that had begun in the Gulf of Tonkin two days earlier, running 80 nautical miles offshore past Quang Ngai, turned west at dusk toward the Phu Yen coast; it did not stop, did not fish and did not change speed. The exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut flagged it at 1940, and the Vietnamese Navy's coastal command at Nha Trang sent a PC-461 from her patrol line off Tuy Hoa. The radar held the trawler for seven hours, so the patrol craft did not have to search; she had only to steam. She came up with the trawler at 0310 about four miles off Vung Ro Bay, under a low overcast and in a moderate northeast swell. The trawler's crew ran her toward the beach and fired her demolition charges, as Group 759's standing orders required; the forward hold blew, the after hold did not. A boarding party pulled out about 30 tons of cargo before the fire reached it: Chinese Type 56 carbines and assault rifles, B-40 (RPG-2) launchers and their rockets, 82mm mortar rounds and about 400,000 rounds of 7.62mm ammunition. Seven of the crew were taken from the water, two of them wounded; nine died in the explosion or drowned. The Vietnamese lost one sailor killed and four wounded to small-arms fire from the beach, where a VC reception party had waited with porters¹⁴⁵.

By the end of June the orbit had tracked eleven contacts that the cell judged to be trawlers, on the II Corps coast and on the Ca Mau peninsula, where the landings went into the mangrove creeks of the Bo De and the Cua Lon. Four were intercepted: two scuttled or blew up, one was taken nearly intact off Binh Dinh in April by the destroyer USS *Edsall*'s boarding party with a Vietnamese liaison officer, and one ran into the Bo De ahead of a junk division and landed most of its cargo. Six turned back when a patrol came over the horizon, which the radar saw as a reversal of course at about the time the trawler's own radar would have detected the patrol. One landed unobserved in May, on a night when the orbit's aircraft had diverted to Clark with a generator fault and the relief was four hours late¹⁴⁵. The casualty figures above are counts of bodies and prisoners and are reliable; the cargo tonnages are estimates from what was recovered and from the prisoners' statements of their loads, and carry a margin of about a third¹⁴⁶.

The prisoners told the rest. Interrogated at Nha Trang, the crew of the April capture described the landing points, the signal lamps, the reception parties and about two years of earlier voyages, and the Vietnamese Navy found two of the landing beaches in Phu Yen and Khanh Hoa from their directions¹⁴⁵. TIDE TABLE did not starve the Front; the tonnage it stopped in six months was a small fraction of what moved by sea and through Laos. What it ended was the cheapest and most direct route by which heavy weapons reached the coastal provinces of II Corps and the lower Delta, and it ended it by being seen, which is the subject of the next event in this chapter.

#### Khanh's coup
*30 Jan 1964 · Saigon · changed from history · EV000-1*
Before dawn on 30 January 1964 Major General Nguyen Khanh, commanding I Corps, took power from the Military Revolutionary Council of General Duong Van Minh without firing a shot. He acted with Generals Tran Thien Khiem and Nguyen Van Thieu and Colonels Nguyen Chanh Thi and Do Mau. Generals Tran Van Don, Le Van Kim, Ton That Dinh and Mai Huu Xuan were put under house arrest on a charge of plotting a French-backed neutralist settlement, and Prime Minister Nguyen Ngoc Tho was detained. The one death was Major Nguyen Van Nhung, Minh's aide, executed after the takeover. Khanh kept Minh as a figurehead chief of state. The generals were tried in closed session on 28–29 May and found guilty only of "lax morality"¹⁴⁷.

The coup was not caused by the decrees of 1 January; it was the product of the generals' rivalries and Khanh's ambition, and it came on the historical date. But the reforms shaped what followed. Officers of landed families and commanders who drew ghost pay gave Khanh their quiet support in the hope of a government that would let the decrees lapse. In February Khanh asked Commissioner Khoa to bring corruption charges against the Dalat generals; Khoa replied that the Commission had no evidence against them and would bring no case without it. Khanh moved to replace him, and the American co-chair of the Aid Compliance Board refused the concurrence the decree required¹⁴³. The generals went before a military court instead, on the "lax morality" charge of the historical record.

Washington was surprised but quick to back the new man; President Johnson sent Khanh a personal note on 2 February¹⁴⁷, which in this war carried the condition, at the urging of the US Mission and this headquarters, that the United States would support no government that repealed either decree¹⁴³. Khanh, who needed American backing more than he needed the landlords, accepted it and kept both. The coup mattered less for what Khanh did than for the precedent it set: a second violent change of government within three months, carried out by officers against officers, and accepted by the United States. Khanh moved, as every coup-maker had, to place his men in the provinces, and the decree of 1 January now stood in his way. In March, Secretary of Defense McNamara visited and endorsed Khanh, and NSAM 288 of 17 March 1964 committed the United States to the survival of an independent, non-communist South Vietnam. In June 1964 General William C. Westmoreland, Harkins's deputy since January, took command of MACV, and General Maxwell D. Taylor replaced Henry Cabot Lodge as ambassador in July.

In this war Khanh's coup was the first test of the police and provincial decrees, and it came thirty days after them, before the Board had published a list. On the morning of 30 January Khanh's officers detained the new Director General of National Police, Vo Dinh Cuong, as they had detained the police chief on the historical course, and in the first week of February Khanh named "acting" chiefs in eleven provinces and districts. The US Mission and this headquarters treated the two decrees as part of the condition Johnson's note attached to the land and anti-corruption decrees: the Aid Compliance Board gave notice that a province whose chief was not taken from the list would receive no counterpart funds once the first list was published, and Cuong was released on 2 February and restored to his office. Khanh kept the Board, because he had no candidate for its chairmanship and saw a reputation for reform in it, and kept his acting chiefs until the list replaced nine of the eleven in April and May; the other two placed well enough to be appointed from it¹⁴⁴. It was a partial failure of the reform's first test and a partial success, and it set the pattern that held until 1970: the Board survived each government because the government that inherited it found it cheaper to keep than to break.

#### The first posts and the Front's answer
*1 Mar 1964 – 31 Jul 1965 · The Delta along the Bassac and the Mekong, the provinces around Saigon, and the coastal Centre from Quang Nam to Binh Dinh · new · EV002-2*
The first village police post was settled at Phu Tan in An Giang on 1 March 1964, in the Hoa Hao country where the titling teams would begin six weeks later, and the posts thereafter followed the titling line into villages the government held at night. Each was a brick house with a radio, an AN/PRC-10 or one of the HT-1 village sets, ten constables armed with M1 carbines and a Thompson or a BAR, and, by the programme's rule, a Popular Forces platoon in the same village. About 600 posts were manned by the end of 1964 and about 1,100 by the end of July 1965. Behind them came the census teams, registering families house by house and issuing the card and the household booklet, and the night check, in which a constable and a PF corporal walked the hamlet after dark with the booklets and counted heads⁶. By July 1965 about 45 percent of the accessible adult population was registered¹⁴⁴.

The Front read the programme within weeks, as this headquarters had expected. A COSVN directive of March 1964, captured in Dinh Tuong in October, named the rural police a target above the Popular Forces, ordered the district committees to kill constables in their first months before they learned the village, to put agents into the Special Branch while it was recruiting fast, and to confiscate the new identity cards in the villages the Front reached at night. On the night of 3 October 1964 a local-force company overran the post at Long Dinh on Route 4 in Dinh Tuong, which had been settled three weeks earlier without its PF platoon, and killed seven of its nine constables; their carbines armed a guerrilla platoon that was still carrying them when it was destroyed in 1966. By the end of July 1965 about 85 posts had been overrun or abandoned, about 410 constables killed and about 300 weapons lost, almost all in posts settled ahead of night security or without the PF platoon the rule required. Of the posts attacked that had their platoon and a reaction force within an hour by road or water, about four in five held¹⁴⁴.

The Special Branch's first harvest came in the held belt. By July 1965 its district sections had arrested about 1,900 people as members of the infrastructure, each on three independent sources, and the magistrates had released about a third of them within the 72 hours, which the Branch resented and this headquarters did not. In February 1965 the Branch found its first penetration, a clerk in the Dinh Tuong provincial section who had been copying informant cards for the province committee's security section since the previous summer; eleven informants named on them had been killed¹⁴⁴. The posts, the census and the files were working where the government held the night and were a list of targets where it did not, which is what the programme's design had said and what the casualty returns now showed.

#### The first titling teams
*16 Mar – 31 Dec 1964 · An Giang, Phong Dinh, Vinh Long, Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An Provinces, and the coastal Centre from Quang Nam to Binh Dinh · new · EV001-2*
The first 40 mobile titling teams of about 25 men each took the field on 16 March 1964, working outward from province capitals and main roads into villages the district could reinforce by day, each escorted by a Regional Forces company armed with M1 and M2 carbines and M1918A2 automatic rifles. The first titles were handed over at Long Xuyen in An Giang on 21 April, in the Hoa Hao country where the Front was weakest, and the first in a contested district at Phung Hiep in Phong Dinh on 11 May. By July 70 teams were in the field, the most the provinces could escort without stripping their daytime reaction forces. They moved by road, in the LCM-6 and LCVP craft of the River Assault Groups and by hired sampan, and to villages beyond both by CH-21C and UH-1B helicopters when the operations staff would release them¹⁴³.

By 31 December 1964 titles had been issued for about 210,000 hectares to about 170,000 families, about a fifth of the target, almost all of it in hamlets held by the government at night or contested only lightly: An Giang, the riverine districts of Phong Dinh and Vinh Long along the Bassac, the inner districts of Gia Dinh and Bien Hoa, and the coastal plain around Hoi An and Tam Ky in Quang Nam. The rent stop held at the main-season harvest of December 1964 in most districts the government controlled; landlords' agents collected under Regional Forces escort in parts of Vinh Long, Vinh Binh and Dinh Tuong, which led to the first suspension a few months later. Twenty-three team members were killed by mines and ambush during the year¹⁴³. The figure the Mission reported to Washington each month was titles issued; this headquarters reported it with the warning that it measured the government's paperwork, not the peasants' allegiance.

#### The dawn musters
*7 Apr – 31 Dec 1964 · ARVN 7th, 9th and 21st Divisions and the Regional and Popular Forces of the Delta, then countrywide · new · EV001-4*
The Pay and Muster Verification Service's first 20 teams began unannounced dawn musters of the ARVN 21st Division's 31st, 32nd and 33rd Regiments around Bac Lieu and Soc Trang on 7 April 1964, and of the 7th and 9th Divisions in Dinh Tuong and Vinh Long in May; by the autumn 60 teams were mustering about 480 units a month across all four corps. Each man was enrolled on an identity card with his photograph and fingerprints, and from August the Service's paymasters paid the enrolled units of IV Corps at the parade rather than through their commanders. By 31 December about 41,000 names had been struck from the rolls of a force carried at about 514,000, about 8%; the rate ran from about 4% in the Airborne and Marine battalions to about 15% in some Regional Forces companies of the Delta¹⁴³. These are counts of men present against names on the roll, not estimates, and they are the best figures this headquarters had ever had for the army it advised.

Washington read them as a smaller army. Secretary McNamara, visiting in May, was briefed on the first returns and told the President that the RVNAF's real strength was below its reported strength by a margin that would grow as the musters spread. In the units mustered twice, the second muster found few ghosts, because commanders had struck them before the teams came. The Commission opened 412 cases by the end of the year, almost all against company and battalion commanders and province finance officers, and brought 61 to trial¹⁴³.

#### The Can Giuoc killings
*9 Jun – 14 Jul 1964 · Can Giuoc and Can Duoc Districts, Long An Province · new · EV001-3*
In May 1964 titling teams from Tan An began work in the villages of Can Giuoc and Can Duoc, the rice districts of Long An between Saigon and the sea, where the survey of December 1963 had found most of the strategic hamlets destroyed and the Front's infrastructure intact. The province chief, under pressure from Saigon for a showcase close to the capital, sent the teams into hamlets the government held by day only and posted the claimants' names in the village offices, as the procedure then required¹⁴³. Each team worked under the escort of a Long An Regional Forces company that withdrew to its post at dusk.

On the night of 9 June armed propaganda teams of the Can Giuoc district committee, with the Long An provincial security section and screened by a company of the provincial 506th Local Force Battalion, entered three hamlets of Phuoc Lai village, read out names from the posted list, and shot four men who had claimed titles and the village land committee's secretary. Over the following five weeks the same pattern was repeated in eleven hamlets of the two districts: by 14 July 37 title holders and 9 members of village land committees had been killed, and several hundred families had brought their titles back to the district office at Can Giuoc rather than keep them in the house. The Regional Forces companies, posted in mud forts along Route 5 and the canal, did not move at night, and no reaction force came. The figures are this headquarters' count from district and province reports, cross-checked by the American province adviser, and are a floor¹⁴³.

The killings were the land programme's first lesson in what it did not change. Teams stopped posting names in hamlets not held at night, and titles in contested hamlets were thereafter held at the district office until the hamlet was secured or the owner asked for his. A COSVN directive captured near Duc Hoa in August told district committees not to obstruct the titling, to let the peasants "take the puppets' paper", to keep collecting the Front's agricultural tax, and to make examples only where a hamlet's people began to inform¹⁴³. In Can Giuoc the titling was suspended until the following spring. It was the first evidence that the reform's value in any district was exactly the value of the security behind it.

#### The battle of Nam Dong
*6 Jul 1964 · Nam Dong CIDG camp, about 50 km west of Da Nang near the Laotian border · historical · EV000-2*
At 02:30 on 6 July 1964 a force that the Americans estimated at about two battalions of Viet Cong and PAVN, some 800–900 men, attacked the CIDG camp at Nam Dong in the mountains west of Da Nang, near the Laotian border. The camp was held by Special Forces Detachment A-726 under Captain Roger H. C. Donlon, 12 Americans in all, with one Australian warrant officer of the Australian Army Training Team Vietnam, 311 Montagnard strikers, 50 Nung mercenaries and seven Vietnamese Special Forces. The attack opened with mortar fire on the command post and a sapper assault on the main gate; the outer perimeter was penetrated and many strikers deserted or went over to the attackers¹⁴⁸.

The inner perimeter, held by the Americans and the Nungs, survived about five hours of close fighting until the attackers withdrew at dawn. Two Americans and the Australian adviser were killed; 58 strikers and Nungs died and 57 were wounded, and more than 100 strikers defected. Fifty-five to sixty-two attackers' bodies were counted around the wire, a figure more reliable than most body counts because the dead lay inside a perimeter the defenders held. Donlon, wounded several times, became the first American awarded the Medal of Honor in Vietnam¹⁴⁸. Nam Dong is the pattern of the border camp war: an isolated garrison of irregulars whose loyalty is uncertain, a night assault by a force that has chosen its moment, no reinforcement before daylight, and a defence that survives on the quality of a handful of men.

#### ◆ The Gulf of Tonkin incidents and the Southeast Asia Resolution
*2–11 Aug 1964 · Gulf of Tonkin and Washington · changed from history · EV000-3*
On 30–31 July 1964 South Vietnamese commando boats under the covert OPLAN 34A programme, run by MACV's Studies and Observations Group, raided the North Vietnamese islands of Hon Me and Hon Ngu. On 2 August the destroyer USS *Maddox*, on a DESOTO signals intelligence patrol under Captain John J. Herrick, was attacked in daylight by three P-4 torpedo boats of the 135th Torpedo Squadron. The *Maddox* fired, four F-8 Crusaders from USS *Ticonderoga* attacked the retiring boats, and the North Vietnamese lost four killed and six wounded with three boats damaged; the *Maddox* was struck by one machine-gun round and suffered no casualties. Johnson, as historically, chose a warning rather than retaliation, and ordered the patrol resumed with USS *Turner Joy* in company¹⁴⁹.

At Admiral Sharp's request I moved the Global Hawk orbit from the II Corps coast to the Gulf on 3 August. There were no SA-2s in the North and nothing that could reach 60,000 feet, and the aircraft took station about 40 nautical miles east of Hon Me, with the destroyers, the coast from Hon Matt to the mouth of the Song Ma and the open Gulf to the east in its radar's maritime mode¹⁰⁵. On the night of 4 August the *Maddox* and *Turner Joy*, in rough weather with thunderstorms, fired for about two hours at radar contacts they took to be a second attack. The watch in the exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut that night was Major Harold Kessling's. His radar log showed the two destroyers and their manoeuvres, a cluster of fishing junks about 30 nautical miles to the west, rain cells moving across the Gulf, and no fast small craft within 40 nautical miles of the ships at any time between 2000 and 2330 local. P-4 boats making attack runs at 40 knots are good radar targets even in a sea state of three and four, and the radar had tracked the three boats of 2 August from their base on the same set. Herrick cabled that the "entire action leaves many doubts"¹⁴⁹. Kessling's report reached MACV at about midnight Saigon time and CINCPAC an hour later, which in Washington was the late morning of 4 August, while McNamara and Sharp were still trying to establish what had happened¹⁴⁵.

What followed was Washington's decision, and it went the other way from history. Historically Johnson had decided on retaliation within thirty minutes of the first reports¹⁴⁹. Here McNamara told him in the early afternoon that the ships' reports were contradicted by a radar that had watched the whole engagement, that Herrick himself doubted them, and that the Navy could not confirm an attack. Johnson held the strike order and, at about 1900, cancelled it. Operation Pierce Arrow was not flown. The 64 sorties against the torpedo-boat bases and the Vinh oil storage did not happen; Lieutenant (jg) Richard Sather was not killed over Hon Gai, and Lieutenant (jg) Everett Alvarez was not shot down and taken prisoner that day¹⁴⁵.

The resolution was sent anyway. Johnson had carried a draft since May, an election was three months away, and the attack of 2 August was real. On 5 August he asked Congress for authority to repel armed attack against US forces and to prevent further aggression, citing the 2 August attack on a US destroyer in international waters and Hanoi's support of the war in the South, and he said nothing of the night of the 4th. The debate ran three days longer than history's: Senator Wayne Morse, tipped about the 34A raids, asked why the destroyer had been near Hon Me at all, and Senator Gaylord Nelson's amendment against a land war was again declined by Senator Fulbright on the administration's assurance that no such war was intended. Congress passed the Southeast Asia Resolution on 10 August, 414–0 in the House and 85–3 in the Senate, in the words of the historical text¹⁴⁵. Johnson signed it on 11 August. It was again the only legislative authority for the war that followed, but it rested on one attack that had happened rather than on two of which one had not, and the radar log of 4 August sat in the files at Tan Son Nhut and Honolulu as a record of a false alarm, not as the concealed proof of a pretext. B-57B bombers were moved to Bien Hoa in the aftermath¹⁵⁰, within mortar range of the countryside around it.

#### The Vung Tau Charter crisis and the September coup attempt
*16 Aug – 14 Sep 1964 · Saigon, Hue and Da Nang · changed from history · EV000-4*
Khanh tried to use the Tonkin crisis to make himself president under a new constitution, the Vung Tau Charter of 16 August 1964. Students and the Buddhist movement led by Thich Tri Quang answered with demonstrations and riots in Saigon, Hue and Da Nang, and Khanh conceded the repeal of the charter. At a Military Revolutionary Council meeting on 26–27 August he promised to resign, but the generals could agree on no successor, and a triumvirate of Khanh, Minh and Khiem was formed to rule for two months; Khanh dominated it. Khiem was sent abroad as ambassador to Washington and Minh on a "goodwill tour"¹⁵¹.

Before dawn on 13 September Generals Lam Van Phat and Duong Van Duc, with about ten battalions recruited among them and four moving up from the Mekong Delta, seized key points in Saigon and announced a new government on the radio while Khanh took refuge in Da Lat. The coup collapsed within a day. US officials publicly backed Khanh; Air Commodore Nguyen Cao Ky, commanding the Vietnamese Air Force, flew jets low over the city and brought loyal marines and paratroopers into Tan Son Nhut, and General Nguyen Chanh Thi supported him; Phat and Duc backed down on the morning of 14 September. No one was killed¹⁵¹. In this war the coup-makers had a programme as well as grievances. General Duc, the IV Corps commander, had learned in August that the Commission had opened a case on the ghost payrolls the musters had found in his corps, and Phat's broadcast of 13 September promised to "suspend the land decree pending review" and to dissolve the "foreign-controlled" Aid Compliance Board. It was the first time a coup had announced a policy toward the countryside, and the policy was the landlords'; the same broadcast promised to "restore the authority of the corps commanders over the provinces", which meant the abolition of the Selection Board¹⁴⁴. Its failure within a day attached the reforms to the side that won: the Young Turks who had saved Khanh took up the land decree as their own cause, and Ky spoke of it in his first public statements as an argument for their claim to govern¹⁴³.

The lasting result was a new power centre: the younger officers, the "Young Turks" around Ky and Thi, who had saved Khanh and now knew they could unmake him¹⁵¹. The war in the countryside was left to commanders whose first concern was the next coup.

The police played a part in this coup that it had never played before. The Special Branch, which watched the generals under its statute as well as the Front, reported to Cuong on 11 September that battalions of the 7th Division were being readied at My Tho without orders, and Cuong passed the report to Khanh and to the US Mission the same night. The warning did not stop the coup, which came on schedule, but on 13 September the Saigon police stayed in their stations and the Field Force companies in their barracks, on Cuong's order, rather than joining either side, and the Mission's knowledge of the movement two days in advance let Ambassador Taylor tell the coup-makers within hours that the United States would not recognise them¹⁴⁴. A police that was not one faction's instrument had raised the cost of a coup by a little, and the plotters' programme of abolishing the Board had made the Young Turks who defeated them its defenders, as it had made them the land decree's.

#### The Hop Tac programme
*Sep 1964 – mid-1965 · Saigon and the surrounding provinces · changed from history · EV000-5*
In September 1964, at the urging of the US Mission and Westmoreland, the government launched Hop Tac ("cooperation"), a plan to pacify a ring of territory around Saigon outward in phases, the "oil spot" concept applied to the capital region. The priority was explicit: concentrate resources on the Saigon area and accept little progress elsewhere for the time being. By then the trend elsewhere was plain. Ambassador Taylor's mission reported on 6 October that the provinces rated unsatisfactory for pacification had risen from 7 in July to 14 in August, and named the diversion of resources to Hop Tac as one possible cause. The mission nonetheless recommended unanimously that Hop Tac be given full support¹⁵².

The programme never reached its schedule. Its ARVN units were repeatedly pulled away by coups and coup alerts, its Vietnamese chain of command changed with each government, and the Viet Cong infrastructure in Gia Dinh, Long An and the provinces north of the capital was left largely intact. Hop Tac carried the titling teams into the inner ring of Gia Dinh and Bien Hoa, and by mid-1965 about 38,000 hectares there had been titled; in the hamlets of the inner ring the Front's tax collectors found fewer families willing to pay without coercion, and the province advisers' reports of volunteered information rose from a trickle to a few dozen reports a month¹⁴³. The provincial reform reached the ring at the same time: Gia Dinh, Bien Hoa and Long An received province chiefs from the list between May and July 1964, and about 160 police posts were settled in the inner districts by mid-1965, with a Special Branch section in each district town. In the inner ring of Gia Dinh the Special Branch broke two of the Front's village committees and the courier line that ran from the Hoc Mon rice fields into the city's northern wards, and the hamlets around the posts were held at night through the coups of 1964–65 even when the ARVN battalions assigned to Hop Tac were pulled away¹⁴⁴. That did not save the programme. By mid-1965 Hop Tac was effectively moribund. Its failure foreshadowed the problem of the whole war: pacification required a stable government and a security force that would stay in the hamlets at night, and in 1964 the Republic of Vietnam had neither.

#### Hanoi commits the regulars; the Trail and the first air war in Laos
*Sep 1964 – Apr 1965 · North Vietnam, the Laotian panhandle and the Central Highlands · changed from history · EV000-6*
The Ninth Plenum of the Lao Dong Party Central Committee, in December 1963, had resolved to seek a decisive military victory in the South before the United States could intervene in strength. Through 1964 the Politburo under Le Duan acted on it. Infiltration down the Trail, managed since 1959 by the PAVN's Group 559, shifted from regrouped southerners to northern-born conscripts, and in the autumn of 1964 the first complete PAVN regular regiments were ordered south. The 95th Regiment of the 325th Division arrived in Kontum Province in December 1964; the 101st Regiment followed in February 1965 with the division commander, Colonel Nguyen Huu An; the 18th Regiment came a few months later. The division was then broken up among the B3 (Central Highlands) Front and Military Region 5¹⁵³. These were the first regular North Vietnamese formations committed to the war, and by the US estimate of 1965 about 4,500 PAVN troops and 300 tons of materiel moved through Laos each month¹⁵⁴.
