The decision of 31 March stands, and the 14,592 Americans killed by hostile action in the year are not reduced by anything that flies at 60,000 feet.

## §4.P4 Phase IV — Vietnamization and drawdown (Jan 1969 – Dec 1971)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: POP.night_control +1 · POP.gvn_legitimacy +1 · VCI.strength -1 · FIND.main_force +1 · FIND.vci +1.*

Phase IV is the war of the American withdrawal. US strength peaks at about 539,000 in the first half of 1969 and falls to 474,000 at the end of that year, 336,000 at the end of 1970 and 158,000 at the end of 1971. American deaths by hostile action fall with it, from about 9,414 in 1969 to 4,221 in 1970 and 1,380 in 1971¹⁴². The Republic of Vietnam's armed forces grow to replace them: from about 897,000 in 1969 to 968,000 in 1970 and about 1,048,000 in 1971–72, the territorial Regional and Popular Forces growing fastest¹⁹⁹. The policy has a name, Vietnamization, and a premise: that the South, given equipment, time and American air power, can hold what the Americans leave.

The shape of the phase is a race between the withdrawal and the South's improvement, run while Hanoi rebuilds. In the countryside, the years 1969–71 are the best the government ever has. The VC, gutted by the 1968 offensives, return to protracted war on a reduced scale; pacification spreads under Phoenix and the territorial forces, into a countryside where the land programme of 1964 has already done most of its work (the Land to the Tiller law of 1970 does not happen, EV000-65); and by most indicators, including the ones the enemy cannot fake, the VC's hold on the population of the Delta and the coastal lowlands weakens. On the frontiers the war goes the other way. The North rebuilds its main forces, extends the Trail and its pipeline, and after Sihanouk's fall loses Sihanoukville but gains much of eastern Cambodia. The United States uses the time it has to strike the sanctuaries it had left alone, secretly from the air over Cambodia from March 1969, on the ground in Cambodia in May–June 1970, and through the ARVN alone in Laos in February–March 1971, and Congress closes the ground option behind each attempt.

At home the war's support is gone. The revelation of My Lai, the Moratorium, the Cambodian incursion and Kent State, the trial of Calley and the publication of the Pentagon Papers mark a country that wants its army home, and an army in Vietnam, after 1969, that is itself coming apart under drugs, racial conflict and attacks on its own officers, waiting to leave a war it no longer expects to win. In Hanoi, Ho Chi Minh dies in September 1969; the collective leadership around Le Duan negotiates secretly with Kissinger and, after the ARVN's defeat in Laos in 1971, decides to try once more for victory by conventional invasion.

In this war the phase follows the same course on the frontiers and in Washington. In the countryside it starts further on. The land programme has nothing left to do by 1970 but the communal lands, and about 1,150,000 hectares are titled by the end of 1971. The Commission Against Corruption, which had outlived three governments behind the American lever, loses that lever as the Americans leave, and in July 1971 Thieu puts it under a presidential inspectorate after it opens an inquiry that reaches his own circle¹⁴³.

The police and the provinces end the phase as the Commission does: their work below the palace goes on, and their reach upward ends as the American lever lapses. The historical gap narrows, because the Republic builds after 1968 what this war had from 1964.

The enemy's rear is counted through the whole phase. The Cambodian incursion goes in on a map five years in the making and finds the caches in days rather than weeks, though Washington still names COSVN as the objective; Commando Hunt's claims are checked every night against a radar count, and the claim that the trucks destroyed exceeded the trucks that existed never survives a season; and Lam Son 719 goes in with an honest estimate of what waits for it, and fails for the reasons it failed historically.

#### Nixon takes office; Vietnamization
*20 Jan – Mar 1969 · Washington and Saigon · historical · EV000-56*
Richard Nixon was inaugurated on 20 January 1969 with a promise to end the war with honour and no published plan to do it. His national security adviser, Henry Kissinger, commissioned a survey of the agencies' views of the war, which showed deep disagreement about almost everything except that the South could not yet stand alone. At a National Security Council meeting on 28 January the Secretary of Defense, Melvin Laird, proposed calling the policy "Vietnamizing" the war; its two parts were to strengthen the RVNAF to take over the fighting and to extend pacification. Henry Cabot Lodge replaced Harriman in Paris¹⁹⁶. Laird, who visited Saigon in March, pressed for withdrawals sooner and larger than the military wanted, and made "Vietnamization" the measure of the South's progress²⁰². The Improvement and Modernization programme behind it re-equipped the RVNAF from top to bottom: M16 rifles for the regulars and then the territorial forces, M60 machine guns, artillery, helicopters, aircraft, and the training base to use them.

#### The post-Tet offensive
*23 Feb – Mar 1969 · Saigon, Da Nang and installations throughout the South · historical · EV000-57*
On the night of 22–23 February 1969 the PAVN and VC opened a countrywide offensive of attacks by fire and sapper raids against more than a hundred targets, including Saigon, Da Nang, Hue and US bases, the heaviest since 1968. It was smaller in scale and ambition than Tet: the ground attacks were mainly against bases and firebases, not into the cities, and the population did not rise. It cost the Americans heavily in the first weeks, and it was taken in Washington as a breach of the understanding that had accompanied the bombing halt. Nixon's answer, in the third week of March, was the secret bombing of the Cambodian base areas²⁰³.

#### Operation Menu
*18 Mar 1969 – 26 May 1970 · Base Areas 350, 351, 352, 353, 609 and 740, eastern Cambodia · historical · EV000-58*
On the night of 18 March 1969, 60 B-52s took off from Guam on a mission ostensibly against targets in South Vietnam; 48 were diverted into Cambodia against Base Area 353 in the Fishhook, where COSVN was believed to be, and dropped about 2,400 tons that night. The campaign that followed, codenamed Breakfast, Lunch, Snack, Dinner, Supper and Dessert after the base areas it struck, flew about 3,800 B-52 sorties and dropped 108,823 tons until 26 May 1970. It was concealed by dual reporting: an overt channel ordered strikes near the border inside South Vietnam, while a back channel ordered the strikes in Cambodia, whose records were destroyed after each mission; neither the Secretary of the Air Force nor its chief of staff was told²⁰³.

Sihanouk, whose territory it was and whose government had tolerated the base areas and the supply line from Sihanoukville, did not protest publicly. The *New York Times* reported the bombing on 9 May 1969; Nixon ordered the leak traced, and the telephones of 13 officials and four journalists were tapped, the first of the abuses that led to Watergate. The bombing forced the PAVN and VC to disperse and camouflage their Cambodian bases but did not stop their offensives, and its effect on the war in the South is disputed²⁰³.

#### Hamburger Hill
*13–20 May 1969 · Ap Bia Mountain (Hill 937), A Shau valley, Thua Thien Province · historical · EV000-59*
In May 1969 the 3rd Brigade of the 101st Airborne Division (Airmobile), under the division commander Major General Melvin Zais, went into the A Shau valley in Operation Apache Snow to destroy the PAVN base areas there. On Ap Bia Mountain, Hill 937, about 1.9 km from the Laotian border, the 3rd Battalion, 187th Infantry under Lieutenant Colonel Weldon Honeycutt met the 7th and 8th Battalions of the PAVN 29th Regiment, dug in. Ten days of frontal assaults followed, with the 1st Battalion, 506th Infantry, the 2nd Battalion, 501st Infantry and the ARVN 2nd Battalion, 3rd Regiment joining for the final attack on 20 May. The Americans lost 56 killed, 367 wounded and 7 missing; they counted 630 PAVN dead on and around the hill, many in tunnels, and captured 89 individual and 22 crew-served weapons and 3 prisoners. On 5 June the hill was abandoned²⁰⁴.

"That hill had no military value whatsoever," Zais said. Senators Edward Kennedy, George McGovern and Stephen Young attacked the battle as senseless; on 27 June *Life* published the photographs of 242 Americans killed in one week. Abrams turned from "maximum pressure" to a posture designed to keep American casualties down, and Nixon announced the first troop withdrawal within weeks²⁰⁴.

#### Midway, the first withdrawals and the Nixon Doctrine
*8 Jun – Dec 1969 · Midway Island; Guam; South Vietnam · historical · EV000-60*
On 8 June 1969 Nixon met Thieu on Midway Island and announced the first withdrawal of US troops, 25,000 men, to be completed by the end of August. Elements of the 9th Infantry Division left the Mekong Delta in July, and the whole 3rd Marine Division had left I Corps by the end of the year²⁰². On 25 July, at Guam, Nixon set out what became the Nixon Doctrine: the United States would honour its treaty commitments and provide a nuclear shield, but would look to its Asian allies to provide the manpower for their own defence. Further withdrawals were announced in September and December. For the troops still in Vietnam, the withdrawals made every American death harder to justify. Discipline in the US Army declined through 1969–71; drug use, racial violence and "fragging", attacks on officers and NCOs with grenades, became endemic in rear areas and in some combat units. The withdrawals were faster than the RVNAF's improvement, and they ran on a timetable set by American politics, not by the war.

#### COSVN Resolution 9 and the death of Ho Chi Minh
*Jul – 2 Sep 1969 · COSVN headquarters and Hanoi · changed from history · EV000-61*
In July 1969 COSVN issued Resolution 9, which accepted that the 1968 offensives had not produced the uprising and directed a return to protracted war: small-unit and sapper attacks, economy of force, the rebuilding of the infrastructure, and a campaign against Vietnamization and pacification, while main forces were preserved in the sanctuaries. It was a decision taken in the light of losses and on the movement's own reading of its position, and it set the pattern of the enemy's war in the South until 1971. On 2 September 1969 Ho Chi Minh died in Hanoi. His testament called for the war to continue until the Americans had left and the country was reunified. Power passed to a collective leadership in which Le Duan, as First Secretary, was the strongest figure, with Le Duc Tho, Truong Chinh and Pham Van Dong. Ho's death did not change the policy; it removed the one figure whose authority stood above the factions, and left the militants who had planned Tet in charge.

In this war Resolution 9 is issued on the same date with the same direction. Its section on the countryside is different in one respect. On the historical course the Front could still campaign in 1969 against a government that had not given the peasants their land. Here the government had been titling since 1964, and the resolution's instruction to the Delta's party committees was to stop contesting the titles, to treat a titled hamlet the government held at night as lost for the present, and to concentrate cadres and the reduced tax effort on the hamlets the government held only by day. That was a realistic instruction and a costly admission: it surrendered the issue on which the Front had been built in the Delta, and it left the movement there with nothing to offer the peasant but reunification and the end of the American war¹⁴³.

The resolution's instruction on the infrastructure was the Front's admission of what the police had done to it. It told the party committees of the Delta and the provinces around Saigon to stop keeping cadres resident in villages with settled police posts, to govern them by visiting cadres who slept elsewhere, to concentrate the reduced effort on the hamlets the government held only by day, and to put the movement's best security cadres into penetration of the Special Branch and of the provincial administration rather than into the villages. A movement that had lived among its people since 1960 now visited them, and visiting cadres taxed less, recruited less and governed less¹⁴⁴.

#### The secret Paris channel opens
*4 Aug 1969 – Feb 1970 · Paris · historical · EV000-62*
In June 1969 the National Liberation Front formed the Provisional Revolutionary Government of the Republic of South Vietnam, to claim governmental status at the talks. On 4 August Kissinger began secret talks in Paris with Hanoi's delegation, first with Xuan Thuy, and from February 1970 with Le Duc Tho, a member of the Politburo. The formal sessions at the Hotel Majestic became a forum for public statements; the substance moved to the secret channel. The core issues were fixed from the start and remained so for three years: Hanoi demanded the withdrawal of all US forces and the removal of Thieu as the price of a settlement, and would not discuss the withdrawal of its own forces from the South; Washington would not overthrow its ally¹⁹⁶.

#### My Lai revealed; the Moratorium
*15 Oct – 24 Nov 1969 · Washington and the United States · historical · EV000-63*
On 15 October 1969 the Moratorium brought hundreds of thousands of Americans out against the war in cities across the country, and on 15 November the Mobilization brought a march of several hundred thousand to Washington. On 3 November Nixon appealed on television to "the great silent majority" to support his policy of withdrawal on his terms, and the speech was well received. On 13 November Seymour Hersh published the story of the My Lai massacre, and on 20 November the photographs taken by the Army photographer Ronald Haeberle appeared. On 24 November the Army appointed Lieutenant General William Peers to investigate the cover-up; his report of 17 March 1970 found that at least 175–200 Vietnamese had been killed and condemned the concealment¹⁹⁴. My Lai became, in American opinion, the symbol of the war in the countryside, and in South Vietnam a weapon for the Front's propaganda in a province that had suffered it.

#### The Board and the Directorate in the palace
*Jan 1970 – Sep 1971 · Saigon and the provinces · new · EV002-6*
The Provincial Administration Selection Board had outlived four governments because each had found it cheaper to keep than to break, and because a province chief appointed outside the list could not sign a budget, a title or a counterpart-fund disbursement while the Aid Compliance Board stood behind the rule⁶. By 1970 the American lever was lapsing with the withdrawal, and Thieu, preparing for the election of 1971, wanted the provinces. He did not abolish the Board. As the terms of its two general officers expired in January 1970 he replaced them with officers of his own staff, and from that spring his Prime Minister, General Tran Thien Khiem, appointed the third name on the list for about half the province vacancies and for most of those in the provinces with the largest electorates. The list still excluded the incompetent and the bought; it no longer excluded the loyal mediocre¹⁴⁴.

The Inspectorate's reports, which had gone to the Board and the Commission since 1964, were routed through the presidential Inspectorate General from July 1971 under the decree that subordinated the Commission. The National Police had followed the same road earlier: its Director General had been the President's appointee in fact since 1966, and in 1970–71 the Special Branch's Saigon headquarters spent a growing share of its effort on the opposition, the An Quang Buddhists and the Ky camp, as historical police chiefs always had. The rural posts, the census and the district sections went on much as before, because they were too many and too local for the palace to want them. In September 1971 province chiefs campaigned for Thieu as they did on the historical course; the difference was that about two-thirds of them were men who had passed an examination and served a district first, and the villages they governed were governed better than they were represented¹⁴⁴.

#### Sihanouk falls; the PAVN turns on Cambodia
*18 Mar – Apr 1970 · Phnom Penh and eastern Cambodia · historical · EV000-64*
On 18 March 1970, while Sihanouk was abroad, the Cambodian National Assembly deposed him, and General Lon Nol became the head of a government hostile to the Vietnamese communists. Lon Nol closed the port of Sihanoukville to the supplies that had reached the PAVN and VC base areas through it. From 29 March the PAVN attacked the Cambodian army and within weeks overran much of eastern and northeastern Cambodia, protecting its bases and opening the country to the Khmer Rouge, whom it armed and trained²⁰⁵. Cambodia, a sanctuary since 1965, became a battlefield. The loss of Sihanoukville threw the whole burden of supplying the southern half of South Vietnam onto the Trail, which in turn was extended through Laos and northeastern Cambodia.

#### Land to the Tiller
*26 Mar 1970 · Republic of Vietnam · no longer happens · EV000-65*
The Land to the Tiller law of 26 March 1970 is not enacted in this war, because there is nothing left for it to do. On the historical course it limited holdings of rice land to 15 hectares, gave tenanted land to the tenants who tilled it, confirmed title for peasants who had received land from the Viet Cong, and cost 441 million dollars, 339 million of them American; historians debate whether it came "too little, too late"²⁰⁶. Here the decree of 1 January 1964 had done all of that six years earlier, with a lower ceiling, and by March 1970 about 1,050,000 hectares had been titled to about 800,000 families⁵. What happens instead on 26 March 1970 is a smaller act: Thieu signs a decree converting the remaining provisional titles to final ones and extending the programme to the communal rice lands of the villages, the last large category of tenanted land, about 200,000 hectares let each year by village councils¹⁴³. It draws no headlines in Washington, because the programme it completes had been reporting monthly for six years. Its effects had accrued over those years, not over the last five of the war, in the attitude of the rural population of the Delta towards a government that had given them something before it asked them for anything.

#### ◆ The Cambodian incursion
*29 Apr – 30 Jun 1970 · The Parrot's Beak and the Fishhook, eastern Cambodia · changed from history · EV000-66*
On 29 April 1970 ARVN forces of III and IV Corps under Lieutenant General Do Cao Tri attacked into the Parrot's Beak west of Saigon, and on 1 May the US 1st Cavalry Division and 11th Armored Cavalry Regiment, with ARVN airborne units, attacked into the Fishhook north of it; the 25th and 4th Infantry Divisions followed²⁰⁵. In this war the attack went in on a map. Five years of the Sihanoukville count and the U-2S's radar change detection had located the depots where the convoys stopped, the road net that joined them and the clearings they hid in, and the 1st Cavalry's brigades were landed on the largest of them on the first two days rather than finding them by chance. The great complex that the 1st Cavalry found on 4 May historically, and that the troops called "the City", was its objective on 1 May here¹⁴⁵.

Nixon announced the incursion on television on 30 April as an attack on COSVN. MACV had told CINCPAC and the Joint Chiefs on 24 April that the U-2S had heard COSVN's transmitters move west and north out of the Fishhook from about 19 April, and advised against naming it as an objective. The speech was written in Washington, and it named it¹⁴⁵. COSVN had moved before the attack. The PAVN and VC mostly avoided battle, leaving rearguards, and withdrew westward²⁰⁵.

In the base areas the allies found the logistics of the war in the South: about 22,900 individual and 2,500 crew-served weapons, 7,000–8,000 tons of rice, about 1,800 tons of ammunition, 431 vehicles and 55 tons of medical supplies. Here most of it was found in the first ten days rather than through May and June, and the total was larger by about a tenth, because the depots on the map were searched first and the time left over went to the ones that were not¹⁴⁵. The allies claimed 11,369 PAVN and VC killed and 2,509 captured; I make no adjustment to those claims, which carry the usual inflation. US forces were out by 30 June; the ARVN stayed on²⁰⁵.

On 4 May Ohio National Guardsmen shot dead four students at Kent State University, and protests closed hundreds of campuses. On 30 June the Senate passed the Cooper–Church amendment barring US ground forces from Cambodia. The incursion bought time: the loss of the caches and of Sihanoukville delayed the PAVN's recovery in III and IV Corps, and the southern half of the country was quieter for more than a year. It did not destroy the sanctuary. The PAVN rebuilt its supply routes, seized Attapeu and Saravane in Laos, opened a "Liberation Route" through northeastern Cambodia to the Mekong, and in 1972 would attack An Loc from Cambodia²⁰⁵. The radar watched the Liberation Route being built from the summer of 1970, as it had watched Sihanoukville; it could not stop it either.

#### Fire Support Base Ripcord
*1–23 Jul 1970 · Fire Support Base Ripcord, near the A Shau valley, Thua Thien Province · historical · EV000-67*
In the spring of 1970 the 101st Airborne Division reopened Fire Support Base Ripcord on the eastern edge of the A Shau valley for an offensive against the PAVN base areas. From 1 July the PAVN 324B Division, with its 803rd Regiment at Ripcord, its 29th Regiment on the nearby hills and a battalion of the 6th Regiment of the Tri-Thien command in support, besieged the base, held by the 2nd Battalion, 506th Infantry of the 3rd Brigade under Colonel Ben Harrison. On 18 July a CH-47 hit by small-arms fire crashed into the ammunition storage area, killing four and destroying six M102 howitzers and 2,238 rounds of 105mm ammunition. The command judged the base indefensible and evacuated it on 23 July under fire; the battalion commander, Lieutenant Colonel Andre Lucas, was among the last killed and received the Medal of Honor posthumously. The Americans lost 75 killed and claimed 422 PAVN dead. It was the last major battle between US ground forces and the PAVN. Whether it crippled the PAVN in the region, as Harrison believed, or achieved the PAVN's purpose by forcing the Americans out, is disputed²⁰⁷.

#### ◆ Commando Hunt and the growth of the Trail
*10 Oct 1970 – Dec 1971 · The Laotian panhandle and northeastern Cambodia · changed from history · EV000-68*
The dry-season phases of Commando Hunt were the main US effort against the North's logistics after 1968. Phase V, from 10 October 1970 to 30 April 1971, claimed 16,266 trucks destroyed, a figure Seventh Air Force itself lowered to 11,000; in Phase VII the AC-130E gunships were credited with 7,335 of 10,689 trucks claimed. The CIA estimated that the entire North Vietnamese truck inventory was about 6,000, and a Defense Intelligence Agency model reached zero trucks remaining fourteen times in 1969. Over the campaign the Air Force claimed 51,000 trucks and 3,400 anti-aircraft guns destroyed²⁰¹.

In this war a second ledger stood beside the claims from 1969, and it was kept by the radar. Its Phase V figure was about 4,500–5,500 trucks destroyed or disabled, and its estimate of net loss to the throughput was about 8–10 percent, against the phases' own historical estimate falling to about 2 percent²⁰¹. The DIA model's zero did not reach the Secretary of Defense's desk in 1969 because the radar showed, every night, the trucks that the model said did not exist. The claims went on being made; the AC-130E crews were not lying, and a burning truck at night is easy to count twice. What changed was that no one in Washington or Saigon could believe them for long, and the Seventh Air Force's own reductions came a season earlier¹⁴⁵.

The Trail grew regardless. After the PAVN's offensives in Laos in 1970 its corridor widened from about 30 to about 90 miles; by 1970 a petroleum pipeline from Vinh reached towards the A Shau; the PAVN built more than 3,000 km of roads and paths with bulldozers and graders, and floated fuel drums down the rivers. The U-2S's radar mapped each new road within days of its cutting, and the Global Hawk counted its traffic; the count rose through 1971 to about 150–200 southbound truck movements a night at the dry season's peak¹⁴⁵. It is the clearest case in the war of a metric improving without the war improving: the trucks destroyed rose, and what arrived in the South rose with them. In this war that sentence was written in 1969 by this headquarters, from its own counts, and not by historians. Over the war more than three million tons of ordnance fell on Laos²⁰¹.

#### The Son Tay raid
*21 Nov 1970 · Son Tay prison camp, about 37 km west of Hanoi · historical · EV000-69*
On the night of 20–21 November 1970 a joint task force under Brigadier General LeRoy J. Manor and Colonel Arthur D. Simons landed 56 Special Forces soldiers by HH-53 and HH-3E helicopters, guided by C-130 Combat Talons, at the prison camp at Son Tay to rescue about 61 American prisoners. Navy aircraft from USS *Oriskany* and USS *Ranger* flew a diversion over Haiphong, and F-105G Wild Weasels suppressed the SA-2 sites. The camp was empty: the prisoners had been moved in July, which intelligence learned only days before. The raiders lost two wounded, an HH-3E deliberately crash-landed inside the compound and an F-105G to a SAM; they reported 42 guards killed, a Vietnamese source six guards and seven civilians. The raid failed in its purpose but succeeded in another: Hanoi consolidated its prisoners in central prisons in Hanoi, and many returning prisoners said their treatment and morale improved²⁰⁸.

#### The Cooper–Church amendment
*29 Dec 1970 · Washington · historical · EV000-70*
After the House had rejected the version passed by the Senate in June, the Cooper–Church amendment was enacted in December 1970 as part of the Supplementary Foreign Assistance Act and signed on 29 December. It barred US ground troops and advisers from operations in Laos and Cambodia²⁰⁵²⁰⁹. It wrote into law the sanctuary constraint that had been policy since 1965, at the moment the administration was planning to attack the Trail on the ground. Any operation into Laos would now be the ARVN's alone, without the American advisers who called in the air support and coordinated the helicopters on which ARVN operations depended.

#### ◆ Lam Son 719
*8 Feb – 25 Mar 1971 · Route 9 from Khe Sanh to Tchepone, the Laotian panhandle (Base Areas 604 and 611) · changed from history · EV000-71*
In Dewey Canyon II, beginning at the end of January, US forces reopened Route 9 to Khe Sanh inside South Vietnam. On 8 February ARVN I Corps under Lieutenant General Hoang Xuan Lam attacked west along Route 9 into Laos towards Tchepone, with the 1st Infantry Division, the Airborne Division, the Marine Division, the 1st Armored Brigade and the 21st and 39th Ranger Battalions, to cut the Trail at Base Areas 604 and 611 in the dry season's peak. US Army helicopters flew more than 160,000 sorties in support and B-52s 1,358 sorties with 32,000 tons; no American advisers could go with the ARVN units. The PAVN, which had expected the attack, committed the B-70 Corps with the 304th, 308th and 320th Divisions, and the 2nd and 324B Divisions, with tanks and heavy anti-aircraft artillery²⁰⁹.

In this war the enemy's response was watched as it came, and it made the planning honest without making the operation succeed. From mid-January the U-2S heard the B-70 Corps headquarters come up on the air north of Route 9, and the panhandle watch tracked the 308th Division's vehicles and an armoured column moving south from the Ban Karai roads toward Route 92. MACV J-2's estimate on 4 February put the enemy's strength within reach of Tchepone at about 35,000–40,000 men and rising, about twice the historical estimate given to the planners. Abrams passed it to the Joint General Staff and to Washington. The operation went ahead on its date; its purpose and its timing were set in Washington and the Palace, and the estimate changed neither¹⁴⁵.

The advance stalled within days. Firebases north of Route 9 held by Rangers and Airborne were overrun or abandoned. On 25 February the radar tracked about twenty PAVN tanks, T-54s and PT-76s, forming up north of Fire Support Base 31 at about 0900, and strike aircraft cued on the tracks destroyed six before they reached the hill. The base fell that afternoon all the same, when the forward air controller directing its air support was diverted to a downed F-4 and the remaining tanks came in behind the infantry. On 6 March, in the largest helicopter assault of the war, the 2nd Regiment of the 1st Division was lifted towards Tchepone and entered the abandoned town on 7 March, and from 9 March the withdrawal began. It became a retreat under pressure, with the Marines' Fire Support Base Delta falling on 22 March and the images of ARVN soldiers clinging to helicopter skids reaching the world²⁰⁹. Along Route 9 the radar showed the PAVN columns closing on the retreating units, and the B-52s and tactical aircraft were put on the roads behind them rather than on the jungle beside them¹⁴⁵.

The last ARVN units were out by 25 March; Khe Sanh was abandoned again on 6 April. The Americans lost 215 killed, 1,149 wounded and 38 missing, and 108 helicopters destroyed and 618 damaged. South Vietnamese losses are given from 1,146 to 8,843 killed, a range that is itself a measure of the dispute; Saigon claimed 19,360 PAVN killed and Hanoi admits 2,163 killed and 6,176 wounded. The radar's tracks killed tanks and trucks the PAVN would otherwise have kept, perhaps 40–60 armoured vehicles over the six weeks against a historical toll that is itself disputed, and I do not adjust the ARVN's losses, which were decided by the helicopter landing zones and the command, not by what anyone could see¹⁴⁵. Nixon said on 7 April that "Vietnamization has succeeded". Hanoi read it otherwise, and the traffic on the Trail rose soon after²⁰⁹; the radar counted the rise.

#### The war on trial at home
*29 Mar – 30 Jun 1971 · Fort Benning, Washington and New York · changed from history · EV000-72*
On 29 March 1971 a court-martial at Fort Benning convicted Lieutenant Calley of the premeditated murder of at least 20 people at My Lai and sentenced him to life imprisonment; two days later Nixon ordered him moved to house arrest pending appeal. His sentence was later reduced, and he was paroled in 1974¹⁹⁴. In April veterans opposed to the war demonstrated in Washington and threw their medals onto the Capitol steps. On 13 June the *New York Times* began publishing the Pentagon Papers, the Defense Department's secret history of the war's decisions to 1968, leaked by Daniel Ellsberg; on 30 June the Supreme Court refused to stop publication. The administration's response, the "plumbers" unit formed to stop leaks, led to Watergate. By mid-1971 a majority of Americans told pollsters the war had been a mistake.

In this war the Phoenix programme comes before Congress on the historical date, in the hearings of the House Government Operations subcommittee in July 1971, at which William Colby testified that more than 20,000 people had been killed under Phoenix since 1968. The hearings were damaging and less damaging than they were historically. The Commission's quarterly samples since 1968 gave the administration an independent count of unsupported neutralizations, falling from about a third in 1969 to about a sixth in 1971, and the Special Branch's magistrate review and capped detention gave it a legal frame to describe. The Con Son "tiger cages" of July 1970 had happened as they did historically, in a prison the statute did not reach. The Public Safety appropriation for 1972 passed with a cut of about a tenth rather than the larger cuts its critics proposed, and the identity system's card stock and the Special Branch's American trainers were kept¹⁴⁴.

#### The Commission placed under the Inspectorate
*14 May – 9 Jul 1971 · Saigon · new · EV001-8*
On 14 May 1971 the Commission Against Corruption opened an inquiry into the sale of import licences for fertilizer and motor vehicles under the Commercial Import Program, which its auditors had traced through three Saigon firms to a relative of a member of the President's staff. It was the first inquiry in seven years to reach the presidential palace, and it reached it in the year Thieu stood for re-election. The American lever that had protected the Commission since 1964, the Aid Compliance Board's power to suspend counterpart funds to a named command, importer or province, had not been used since 1969: the Mission was managing a withdrawal, and Washington would not risk a quarrel with the government it was leaving over a licence case. Commissioner Khoa's second term, from January 1970, had been confirmed with the American co-chair's concurrence; his removal still required it⁵¹⁴³.

Thieu did not remove him. On 9 July 1971 he issued a decree placing the Commission, the Special Court's prosecutors and the Pay and Muster Verification Service under a new Inspectorate General of the Presidency, with authority to approve or suspend any inquiry touching an official of the rank of minister, general officer or province chief and above. The licence inquiry was suspended the same day. The Deputy Chief of Mission protested in writing; the Embassy did not invoke the Board. Khoa stayed in office and the Commission's work below that rank went on: the musters, the pay at the parade, the land court cases and the Phoenix audits continued through 1971 at about their earlier rate. What ended was the reach upward that had convicted a general in 1967. An anti-corruption body that had survived seven years with a patron and an American lever lost the lever first, and the patron followed his own interest¹⁴³.

#### Thieu's unopposed re-election
*Oct 1971 · Republic of Vietnam · historical · EV000-73*
In the presidential election of October 1971 Thieu ran alone. Ky was at first disqualified under an election law requiring endorsements from deputies or provincial councillors, then reinstated by the Supreme Court, and withdrew; Duong Van Minh withdrew, charging that the election was rigged. Thieu received all the valid votes, 5,971,114, and the reported turnout was 87.97 percent of 7,192,660 registered voters; valid ballots were 94.37 percent of those cast²¹⁰. A government that had won with a third of the vote in a contested election in 1967 was now returned in a referendum on itself. The army and administration remained Thieu's, and the regime was more stable than at any time since 1963; but its claim to stand for the whole non-communist South had narrowed, at the moment American forces were leaving it to stand alone.

In this war the election runs as it did historically, with the same withdrawals, the same single candidate and the same returns. Its context is different in one respect and the same in another. Thieu's government could claim in the countryside what it could not claim on the historical course, that it had given about 800,000 families their land seven years before the election, and its province chiefs campaigned on it. But the subordination of the Commission in July had removed the one body with the standing to hear the opposition's complaints about the endorsement law, and Minh's charge that the election was rigged was heard in Saigon as a description of a government that had shut down its own inspector in the year it chose to run alone¹⁴³.

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1971 the United States has about 158,000 military personnel in Vietnam, its ground combat role is nearly over, and the last US combat divisions are leaving¹⁴². The RVNAF numbers more than a million men under arms, well equipped and, in the territorial forces, more present in the hamlets than ever before¹⁹⁹. In the countryside the VC remain weaker than at any time since 1960 and weaker in the Delta than on the historical course; the land programme of 1964 has finished its work, with about 1,150,000 hectares titled, and the Land to the Tiller law of 1970 was never needed. The Commission survives below the rank of minister and general and no longer above it¹⁴³. These gains are real, and they are measured mostly by indicators the government itself produces.

On the frontiers the balance is the reverse. The PAVN has rebuilt its main forces, extended the Trail in Laos and Cambodia, lost Sihanoukville and replaced it, and watched the ARVN's best divisions fail in Laos without American advisers. The secret channel in Paris is deadlocked on Thieu's removal and on the PAVN's presence in the South. In Hanoi, after Lam Son 719, the Politburo has decided to stake the next year on a conventional invasion across the DMZ, from Laos into the Central Highlands and from Cambodia towards Saigon, timed to an American election year and to the near completion of the American withdrawal²⁰⁹. The question of Phase V is the one Vietnamization was built to answer: whether the South can stop a PAVN conventional offensive with American air power but without American ground troops.

The Board still ranks the candidates and the Prime Minister now chooses the loyal among the competent¹⁴⁴. The posts, the census and the district Special Branch sections stand, and the historical police has caught up with them in size; what the Republic keeps from 1964 is the chiefs' experience and four extra years of files.

The radar's ledger has made the interdiction debate honest and changed nothing it was about: the Trail is larger at the end of 1971 than ever, the Liberation Route through northeastern Cambodia is mapped and open, and the aircraft that count both will leave with the Americans¹⁴⁵.

### Mechanism

Phase IV is the phase in which the enemy's rear is most fully known and least affected by it. The radar's ledger on the Trail reaches its maturity: by Commando Hunt V it puts the trucks destroyed at about a third of the Air Force's claim and the net loss to throughput at about 8–10 percent, against the historical estimate's fall to 2 percent, and it records the dry-season peak of 1971 at about 150–200 southbound movements a night. The A13 sentence — the trucks destroyed rose, and what arrived in the South rose with them — was written historically by historians; here it was written by this headquarters, from its own counts, in 1969.

The incursion of 1970 is where the measurement of 1965–69 pays off on the ground, and it is the terminal link of the chain that began with the trawlers. The depots the convoys had stopped at for five years were on the map, and the 1st Cavalry landed on the largest of them on its first day. The haul came faster and somewhat larger. Its strategic effect is the historical one, because the strategic effect was set by Lon Nol's closing of Sihanoukville and by the limit of thirty kilometres and sixty days, not by the speed of the search.

Lam Son 719 shows the opposite: an honest estimate that changed nothing. J-2's figure of 35,000–40,000 enemy within reach of Tchepone was twice the one history gave the planners, and it went to the Palace and to Washington before the attack. The operation's date and purpose were political, and they held. On the battlefield the radar killed armour, about forty to sixty vehicles over the six weeks, and its tracks put six tanks out of the attack on Fire Support Base 31 before they reached the hill; the base fell when the air controller was pulled away to a downed F-4. The aircraft improve the strike half of the find-to-fix chain wherever an aircraft is already overhead. They do nothing for an army whose failure is in its landing zones and its command.

### Enemy response and initiative

Hanoi rebuilt through this phase as it did historically, and its learning about the aircraft was complete by now: movement in the open in rain and in the orbit's gaps, the pipeline buried, the Liberation Route through northeastern Cambodia cut where the canopy was thickest. The radar mapped the Liberation Route from the summer of 1970 as it had mapped Sihanoukville, and it could not stop it either. After Lam Son 719 the Politburo concluded, as it did historically, that the ARVN without American advisers could be beaten in the field, and decided to stake 1972 on a conventional invasion. The aircraft saw that army being built. They did not change the decision to build it.

### Causal chains

**C003-4** [war-shaping] The panhandle watch counts trucks into and out of the Trail from November 1968, and MACV sends its net-loss figures to Washington beside the Air Force's claims → by 1969–70 nobody in Washington believes the claims, and the radar shows that air interdiction stops about a tenth of the tonnage at best → the case that only a ground operation can cut the Trail is made in late 1970 partly on this headquarters' own evidence → the ARVN is sent to Tchepone in February 1971 on an argument the radar helped build, against an enemy the radar had measured at twice the planners' historical estimate, and fails for reasons no radar touches: the instrument that made the air war honest becomes one of the arguments for a ground offensive the ARVN could not win.

### Downstream

Phase V inherits a counted Trail at its largest, an enemy army being built in the open for a conventional offensive and seen being built, and an electronic order of battle of the North's air defences seven years deep. It also inherits the fact that the aircraft, their analysts and their exploitation cells are American and will leave with the Americans; nothing of them has transferred to the Republic.

### Clock

The aircraft lengthen the clock slightly in Phase IV, through honest numbers. The truck counts that historically became one of the war's late scandals, the claim of more trucks destroyed than the North owned, never reach Washington uncontested, and the Defense Intelligence Agency's model never reports zero. That removes one item from the list of things Americans discovered they had been told falsely. Against it, the incursion's announcement still named COSVN against this command's advice, the press still found it gone, Kent State still happened, and Cooper–Church still passed. The withdrawal's pace was set by Nixon's politics and Congress's patience, and the aircraft did not touch either. No date in the drawdown moves.

## §4.P5 Phase V — The Easter Offensive and Linebacker (Jan 1972 – Dec 1972)

### Chronicle

*End of phase against the historical state: FIND.main_force +1.*

1972 is the test of Vietnamization, and the answer it gives is exact: the Republic of Vietnam can stop a full PAVN conventional offensive with American air power and American advisers, and cannot do it without them. On 30 March the PAVN opens the largest offensive of the war, called the Nguyen Hue Offensive in Hanoi and the Easter Offensive in the West, with about fourteen divisions and hundreds of tanks on three main fronts: across the DMZ into Quang Tri, from Laos and Cambodia into the Central Highlands at Kontum, and from Cambodia down Route 13 at An Loc towards Saigon. US ground strength has fallen to about 69,000 men, few of them combat troops, and is on its way to 27,000²¹¹. US air power returns in strength: B-52s and tactical aircraft over the battlefields of the South, and, for the first time since 1968, a bombing campaign against the North without most of Rolling Thunder's restrictions, with the mining of Haiphong.

The shape of the phase is a near-collapse in the spring and a recovery in the summer. Quang Tri Province falls on 1–2 May; Kontum and An Loc are besieged and held by narrow margins and B-52s; and by the autumn the ARVN, under better commanders, has retaken Quang Tri City. The PAVN loses most of the armour it committed and, by most estimates, about 100,000 men, killed and wounded, while keeping about a tenth of the South's territory, mostly thinly populated border regions in the west. The offensive creates almost a million refugees and kills more than 25,000 civilians²¹¹. Its political result is the one Hanoi needed: a settlement on terms that leave PAVN troops in the South. The year ends with the October draft agreement, Thieu's refusal of it, and the eleven days of Linebacker II.

In this war the armour of the Easter Offensive is seen before it crosses the DMZ and is struck by radar-bombing B-52s through the cloud that grounded the fighter-bombers; Linebacker chooses from target folders seven years in the making and counts the effect of the mining on the North's own roads; and at An Loc the B-52 boxes of 10–12 May fall on assembly areas the radar shows. Quang Tri still falls, An Loc and Kontum still hold, and the lessons both sides draw are the historical ones.

#### ◆ The Easter Offensive opens: the DMZ front
*30 Mar – 2 Apr 1972 · Quang Tri Province, from the DMZ to the Cua Viet and Camp Carroll · changed from history · EV000-74*
The armour was seen before it crossed. From 12 March the Gulf watch, standing off the Quang Tri coast outside the SA-2s that the North had moved into and just north of the DMZ, tracked tracked-vehicle columns moving south at night on Route 1A and Route 101 from Vinh Linh toward the Ben Hai, and the panhandle watch tracked others moving east along Route 9 in Laos toward Khe Sanh. By 25 March the J-2 count of armoured vehicles in assembly areas within 20 kilometres of the DMZ was about 90–120, and MACV warned I Corps on 27 March of an armoured attack across the demarcation line within days. Lieutenant General Lam, the I Corps commander, expected the main blow from the west, as most of the American and Vietnamese staffs did; the warning reached the 3rd Division, and the rotation of its regiments between firebases, which history records as under way when the attack fell, went ahead in part¹⁴⁵.

At midday on 30 March 1972 PAVN artillery, including 130mm guns that outranged anything the ARVN had, opened on the firebases strung along the DMZ, and the 304th and 308th Divisions with armoured regiments attacked across the demarcation line, while the 324B came in from the A Shau and the west. In all, PAVN forces on the northern front were supported by more than 100 tanks, T-54s, T-34s and PT-76s. The front was held by the ARVN 3rd Division under Brigadier General Vu Van Giai, a new division formed in late 1971 from mixed and unsteady units, with the 147th and 258th Vietnamese Marine Brigades under its control²¹². Low cloud kept most air support away for the first days²¹¹. It did not keep the radar away. The B-52s, which bombed by radar in any weather, were put on the crossing points of the Ben Hai and the roads south of Gio Linh where the Gulf watch showed the armour moving, and on 31 March and 1 April they struck three columns in the open; by our own estimate, about 20–30 armoured vehicles were destroyed or disabled before they reached the firebases¹⁴⁵.

The firebases fell back or were overrun all the same. On 2 April the 56th Regiment at Camp Carroll, under Lieutenant Colonel Pham Van Dinh, surrendered with about 1,500 men. That afternoon at Dong Ha, Captain John Ripley, US adviser to the 3rd Vietnamese Marine Battalion, spent about three hours under fire placing charges on the highway bridge over the Cua Viet, which was blown at 16:30 as PAVN tanks approached; the Marines held the river line²¹². The PAVN halted to regroup and bring up supplies, and the 3rd Division, under fire from guns it could not reach, settled into a defence of Quang Tri City along the Thach Han and Cua Viet rivers. The radar had seen the tanks; it had not made the 3rd Division's regiments steady or the 56th's commander stay.

#### ◆ Freedom Train, Pocket Money and Linebacker
*5 Apr – 23 Oct 1972 · North Vietnam, Haiphong harbour and the approaches to the DMZ · changed from history · EV000-75*
On 5 April Nixon authorized strikes north of the 20th parallel, Operation Freedom Train, and on 10 April B-52s struck petroleum storage near Vinh. On 27 April and 13 May F-4s with laser-guided bombs dropped the span of the Thanh Hoa bridge that Rolling Thunder had failed to bring down, and on 10–11 May the Paul Doumer bridge at Hanoi was dropped the same way¹⁶⁴. On 8 May (9 May in Vietnam) Nixon announced Operation Pocket Money, the mining of Haiphong and the other ports: Navy aircraft laid the mines, which were activated after five days, and about 11,000 more mines were sown in the secondary harbours and waterways over the following days²¹³. The mining of the northern ports, prohibited since 1965, closed the North's main entry point for Soviet supplies by sea. The Gulf watch saw it close: the ships at anchor in the Haiphong roads that did not move, the lighters that tried the channels at night, and from June the trucks massing at the Chinese border crossings at Dong Dang and Lao Cai to replace the sea¹⁴⁵.

Linebacker, from 10 May to 23 October, opened the North to bombing with targets chosen by the commanders rather than in Washington. On its first day, in 414 sorties, the heaviest air-to-air fighting of the war took place, and Lieutenant Randy Cunningham and Lieutenant (jg) William Driscoll became the first American aces of the war. In this war the commanders chose from a target system mapped for seven years: the U-2S's folders of every bridge, rail yard and fuel dump between Hanoi and the DMZ, each with its repair history, and the Gulf watch's nightly count of what was moving on the coastal road and railway, so that the armed reconnaissance south of Thanh Hoa was sent to columns that were moving rather than to roads where traffic had been. Damage was assessed the next day by radar through the summer cloud. Linebacker flew 41,653 missions and dropped 155,548 tons, and lost 134 aircraft, 104 in combat; I estimate the electronic order of battle saved perhaps 10–15 of those losses, and I hold the estimate loosely. Its effect was felt at the front: by a PAVN history only 30 percent of planned supplies reached its front-line units in May and June, the PAVN shelling of the South fell by half between 9 May and 1 June, and by September imports were 35–50 percent below the May level. The radar's count of southbound traffic below Vinh in July was about 40 percent of the March level, which is the same story told by the enemy's own trucks¹⁴⁵. The Soviet Union and China denounced the mining and did nothing; the Nixon–Brezhnev summit in Moscow went ahead from 22 May²¹³.

#### ◆ Loc Ninh and the siege of An Loc
*7 Apr – 18 Jun 1972 · Loc Ninh and An Loc, Binh Long Province, on Route 13 · changed from history · EV000-76*
On 5 April the 5th Division of the PAVN B-2 Front attacked Loc Ninh, on Route 13 near the Cambodian border, and overran it on the afternoon of 7 April; the 9th Division took the Quan Loi base camp that evening, and the 7th Division cut Route 13 south of An Loc at the Tau O bridge with its 141st, 165th and 209th Regiments. An Loc, the provincial capital 100 km north of Saigon, was cut off by 8 April. It was held by the ARVN 5th Division under Brigadier General Le Van Hung, with the 3rd and 81st Ranger Groups, the 1st Airborne Brigade lifted in after failing to break through at Tau O, and the Regional Forces, Popular Forces and People's Self-Defence Force of Binh Long Province, all under III Corps (Lieutenant General Nguyen Van Minh) and advised by the American regional assistance command under Major General James Hollingsworth²¹⁴.

From mid-April, on the nights the single orbit could be spared from the DMZ front and whenever the second aircraft was up, a Global Hawk stood over Binh Long and Tay Ninh and watched the roads out of Cambodia: the PAVN's tanks and trucks coming down Route 13 and the forest tracks from the Fishhook, and the ammunition columns feeding the guns around the town. The 9th Division assaulted on 13 April with tanks; in that first attack the defenders destroyed tanks with M72 LAWs at close range, and tank-killing teams were formed in every battalion. Further assaults followed in April and May. The town survived on airdrops, taken over by USAF C-130s on 19 April once anti-aircraft fire made helicopter landings impossible, and on air support, B-52s above all: on 11–12 May B-52s struck one mission every 55 minutes for 30 hours against the massed PAVN assault, with AC-119 and AC-130E gunships, A-37s and AH-1 Cobras. Here the assault's assembly was on the radar from the evening of 10 May, as tanks and trucks moved up to the rubber north and east of the town, and Hollingsworth's targeteers placed the first B-52 boxes of the night on the assembly areas the radar showed rather than on the ones the prisoners described¹⁴⁵. The 21st Division fought up Route 13 from Lai Khe. The PAVN was driven from the town by 12 June and III Corps declared the siege over on 18 June²¹⁴²¹¹.

US estimates of PAVN losses are 10,000 killed and 15,000 wounded and 36 tanks; Vietnamese figures are about 2,000 killed and 5,000 wounded. I estimate that the radar-cued boxes of 10–12 May added to the PAVN's losses in men and tanks, perhaps by a tenth, and I do not alter the reported figures, which already disagree by a factor of five¹⁴⁵. Brigadier General Richard Tallman, the deputy American commander, was mortally wounded by artillery on 9 July²¹⁴. An Loc lay in ruins. It became the symbol of an ARVN that could hold, if the B-52s came, and in this war of an ARVN that could hold if the B-52s came and the Americans could see where to send them.

#### Tan Canh, Dak To and the battle of Kontum
*24 Apr – 9 Jun 1972 · Tan Canh, Dak To, Ben Het and Kontum city, Kontum Province · historical · EV000-77*
In the Central Highlands the PAVN 320th Division began pressing the firebases on Rocket Ridge in early April, and on 24 April the 2nd Division's 1st and 66th Regiments, with the D10 Sapper Battalion and tanks, overran the ARVN bases at Tan Canh and Dak To II, held by the 42nd and 47th Regiments of the 22nd Division; the division "ceased to exist as a fighting unit", and its commander, Colonel Le Duc Dat, disappeared. The road to Kontum lay open. The PAVN paused for three weeks. John Paul Vann, the civilian director of the II Corps advisory effort, effectively directed the defence; he had Colonel Ly Tong Ba of the 23rd Division put in command of Kontum, and on 10 May Major General Nguyen Van Toan replaced Ngo Du at II Corps²¹¹. On 9 May at Ben Het, ARVN Rangers destroyed three PT-76s with BGM-71 TOW missiles, among the first combat uses of the weapon, and TOW-armed helicopters followed²¹⁵.

The main assault on Kontum came on 14 May, by the 320th Division's 48th, 52nd and 64th Regiments and the 2nd Division²¹¹. B-52 strikes, on 14 May and decisively on the night of 16–17 May, fell close to the ARVN lines and broke the momentum of the attack. From 29 May to 8 June the ARVN cleared the city bunker by bunker, and on 9 June Ba, now a brigadier general, declared Kontum secure. That night Vann's helicopter flew into a hillside in low cloud and he was killed; a Vietnamese account says it was shot down. US estimates put PAVN casualties in the Kontum battle at about 4,000 and in the Highlands campaign at 20,000–40,000, figures with no better basis than the body counts of earlier years²¹⁵.

#### The fall of Quang Tri
*27 Apr – 2 May 1972 · Quang Tri City and Highway 1 south to the My Chanh River · historical · EV000-78*
On 27 April the PAVN renewed its attack on the 3rd Division's defence of Quang Tri City. Contradictory orders from the I Corps commander, Lieutenant General Hoang Xuan Lam, and from Giai left units moving in opposite directions, and on 1 May Giai ordered the city abandoned. The division broke. Soldiers and refugees fled south on Highway 1, and PAVN artillery fired on the column, killing roughly 2,000 soldiers and civilians on the road²¹². Quang Tri Province was lost by 2 May; the Marines who held at the My Chanh River were the one coherent force left north of Hue. On 2 May Thieu relieved Lam and gave I Corps to Lieutenant General Ngo Quang Truong, who stopped the rout, restored order in Hue and held the My Chanh line with the Marine and Airborne Divisions and the 1st Division. Giai was later court-martialled and sentenced to five years' imprisonment²¹¹. The fall of Quang Tri was the first loss of a province to a PAVN conventional offensive, and Truong's recovery the clearest demonstration of what leadership was worth in the ARVN.

#### The recapture of Quang Tri City
*28 Jun – 16 Sep 1972 · Quang Tri Province south of the Thach Han River and the Quang Tri Citadel · historical · EV000-79*
On 28 June Truong opened the counter-offensive, Lam Son 72, with the Airborne Division and the Marine Division under Brigadier General Bui The Lan, whose 147th, 258th and 369th Marine Brigades did most of the fighting for the city. Against them were the 304th, 308th, 320B and 325th Divisions with the 312th identified. US forces provided fire and movement but no ground troops: in July alone, 5,461 tactical sorties and 2,054 B-52 strikes; US Marine helicopters of HMM-164 and HMM-165 lifted the Vietnamese Marines into landing zones, losing several to SA-7 missiles; and the 9th Marine Amphibious Brigade and the Seventh Fleet staged amphibious feints. The PAVN held the Quang Tri Citadel, its walls about 15 feet high, with interlocking tunnels and positions; the Marines breached the southeast wall on 10–11 September, held the Citadel by the late afternoon of 15 September, and raised the flag over the west gate on 16 September after 138 days of occupation. The city was rubble²¹⁶.

The Marine Division lost 977 killed of 3,658 casualties, and the Airborne Division's 2nd Brigade was badly depleted. South Vietnamese claims are 8,135 PAVN killed; Hanoi's are 4,000–10,000. About 20 Americans were killed. The province north of the Thach Han remained in PAVN hands, and both sides were too exhausted to move further²¹⁶. The battle proved that the ARVN's best divisions, under a good commander and with unlimited American fire support, could defeat the PAVN in a set-piece battle. Every one of those conditions was American or rare.

#### The last American ground combat units leave
*Aug 1972 · South Vietnam · historical · EV000-80*
In August 1972 the last US ground combat battalion in South Vietnam, the 3rd Battalion, 21st Infantry of the 196th Brigade, stood down at Da Nang and left the country. American advisers, aviation units, the air and naval forces in and around Vietnam, and a shrinking logistics command remained, and US strength fell to about 24,000 by the end of the year. US deaths by hostile action fell to 300 in 1972¹⁴². The withdrawal begun at Midway in 1969 had been completed for the ground war during the largest PAVN offensive of the conflict, and the defence of the South on the ground had become entirely the RVNAF's. US air power, at its 1972 peak, was the part of the American commitment that remained.

#### The October agreement and Thieu's refusal
*8 Oct – 13 Dec 1972 · Paris, Saigon and Washington · historical · EV000-81*
On 8 October Le Duc Tho presented Kissinger in Paris with a draft that dropped Hanoi's demand for Thieu's removal: a ceasefire in place, the withdrawal of all US forces within 60 days, the return of prisoners, the Thieu government to remain alongside the PRG pending a political settlement through a council of reconciliation, and no requirement for PAVN forces to leave the South. Kissinger accepted it as the basis for an agreement. Thieu rejected it: it left the PAVN in his country and gave the PRG standing, and he had not been consulted in its drafting. On 26 October Kissinger announced that "peace is at hand". On 7 November Nixon was re-elected, carrying 49 states. Washington then sent Saigon a mass of equipment under Operation Enhance Plus, aircraft, armour and artillery, to be in the country before a ceasefire froze replacement at one-for-one, and Nixon gave Thieu private written assurances that the United States would respond with full force to a major violation¹⁹⁶.

In November and December Kissinger brought Saigon's amendments to Paris, Hanoi hardened its own terms in reply, and the main round of talks broke down on 13 December. Nixon, concerned that the new Congress in January would cut off the war's funding, gave Hanoi an ultimatum²¹⁷.

#### Linebacker II
*18–29 Dec 1972 · Hanoi, Haiphong and their surroundings · historical · EV000-82*
On 18 December B-52s attacked Hanoi and Haiphong for the first time in the war. In eleven days, with a 36-hour pause at Christmas, 741 B-52 sorties were dispatched and 729 completed against rail yards at Kinh No, Lang Dang and Yen Vien, power plants including Thai Nguyen, petroleum storage, airfields and SAM sites, dropping about 15,237 tons, and fighter-bombers added about 5,000 tons. The B-52s flew fixed routes and timings on the first nights and suffered for it. North Vietnam fired 266 SA-2s over the eleven days. The United States acknowledged 15 B-52s shot down, and some authors argue for 22–27 counting damaged aircraft written off; 12 tactical aircraft were lost; 33 B-52 crewmen were killed or missing, 33 captured and 26 rescued²¹⁷. By the last nights US commanders judged the North's SA-2 stocks nearly exhausted and its air defences largely suppressed.

North Vietnam's official count of civilian dead is 1,624, 1,328 in Hanoi and 306 in Haiphong. The Bach Mai hospital was struck on 22 December, and on 26 December bombs on Kham Thien Street killed 278 by Hanoi's count. About 80 percent of the North's electrical generating capacity was put out of action. On 30 December Nixon halted the bombing north of the 20th parallel, and Hanoi agreed to resume talks, which reopened on 9 January 1973. Whether the bombing forced Hanoi back to terms or merely covered the American acceptance of terms close to October's is disputed; Le Duan later said it had "completely obliterated our economic foundation"²¹⁷.

#### How the phase ends
At the end of 1972 the war is on the verge of a settlement that both governments in Vietnam regard as provisional. The Easter Offensive has failed to destroy the ARVN or the Thieu government, at a cost to the PAVN of most of the armour it committed (by one count 134 T-54s, 56 PT-76s and 60 T-34s) and about 100,000 men. The ARVN has held at An Loc and Kontum and retaken Quang Tri City, but has lost Quang Tri north of the Thach Han, the western Highlands and the border areas of III Corps, and its losses are given variously from about 10,000 killed to about 39,000 dead in the period. Almost a million South Vietnamese have been made refugees, about 600,000 of them in government camps²¹¹.

The lesson both sides draw is the same. The ARVN held where American air power was decisive, at An Loc and Kontum, and where American advisers coordinated it; it broke where its leadership failed, at Quang Tri and Tan Canh. The settlement about to be signed removes the American advisers and the American air power and leaves the PAVN in the South, while the North's air defences and economy recover. Hanoi needs only to rebuild, wait for the Americans to be gone, and choose its time.

The aircraft are still flying at the end of 1972, still American, and under the agreement about to be signed they will soon have no American strike force to cue. The Republic keeps nothing of them: no aircraft, no exploitation cells, no practice in using either.

### Mechanism

Phase V is where the aircraft's all-weather radar matters most, because the North chose to attack in the spring cloud that had protected it from fighter-bombers in every year of the war. The Gulf watch saw the armour mass north of the DMZ from 12 March, and the B-52s, which bombed by radar in any weather, could be put on the columns at the Ben Hai crossings while the fighter-bombers were grounded. That is a genuine improvement over history, which had low cloud keeping most air support away for the first days: about 20–30 armoured vehicles destroyed or disabled before they reached the firebases, by our estimate.

It did not save Quang Tri, and the reason is the one Part 2 gave for the whole memorandum: the find has outrun the fix, and in 1972 the fix is an ARVN division. The warning of 27 March reached a corps commander who expected the blow from the west and a new division in the middle of a rotation. Camp Carroll's commander surrendered with his regiment. The radar saw the tanks; it could not make the 3rd Division steady. At An Loc the mechanism ran the other way, because there the fix was the B-52s and Hollingsworth's targeteers: the boxes of 10–12 May fell on assembly areas the radar showed, and I estimate they added about a tenth to the PAVN's losses in the decisive assault.

Linebacker is the air war Rolling Thunder should have been, and the aircraft give it seven years of folders and a nightly count of the North's road traffic below Vinh, which fell to about 40 percent of its March level by July. Here the aircraft confirm the enemy's own history of 30 percent of supplies reaching the front, from the enemy's own trucks.

### Enemy response and initiative

 The Easter Offensive was Hanoi's own decision, taken on its own timeline, and it was taken knowing that its armour would be seen. By 1971 the PAVN's General Staff knew from seven years' experience that anything moving in the open on the roads below Vinh and along Route 9 was watched by day and night and in rain. Some on Giap's staff argued for an offensive built for that: infantry and artillery first, the armour held back until the American air was committed elsewhere¹⁴⁵. The Politburo set the date, the axes and the use of armour by other measures: the American election in November, the near completion of the American withdrawal, the lesson of Lam Son 719 that the ARVN could be beaten in the field, and Le Duan's need to show Moscow and Peking, both courting Nixon that spring, that the war would be decided in Vietnam and not in their summits. Those drivers were political and diplomatic, and they owed nothing to anything this command did. The armour crossed the Ben Hai on the date the Politburo chose, into the radar's view, because the date mattered more to Hanoi than the losses.

The enemy's response to the radar in the battle itself was the historical one: move at night, close with the ARVN's positions so that B-52s could not be used, and accept the losses. At An Loc that worked until the assault massed; at Kontum and Quang Tri it worked well enough.

### Causal chains

The phase's chains are the terminal links of chains begun earlier: the electronic order of battle begun in 1965 lands in Linebacker's lower losses, and the radar's all-weather view lands at the Ben Hai crossings. I find no new chain beginning in this phase that reaches a third link not predictable from its first, and I record the shortfall rather than manufacture one. The memorandum's four chains are in Phases I to IV.

### Downstream

Phase VI inherits a PAVN that has lost somewhat more armour and men than history's, and that will rebuild them, as it did historically, in two years of a ceasefire it never observes. It inherits aircraft that are still flying and are about to have no American strike force to cue, and a Republic that keeps none of them. The settlement about to be signed is the historical settlement.

### Clock

Small. The offensive came in an election year with American ground combat nearly over, and the American dead of 1972 were few; the aircraft's contribution, a defence that cost the PAVN somewhat more and Linebacker fewer aircraft, does not touch American tolerance, which by 1972 measured the war by whether the prisoners were coming home and the Americans were leaving. Both happened on history's timetable. The Paris agreement is not hastened or delayed by a radar.

## §4.P6 Phase VI — Paris, the endgame, and 1975 (Jan 1973 – Dec 1975)

 Phase V ends with the enemy's main force better found than history's, and Phase VI does not change for it, because finding was never Phase VI's deficit. Historically Saigon and the Defense Attaché Office knew of the build-up against Phuoc Long and Ban Me Thuot from their own sources. The aircraft are American, and from 15 August 1973 Case–Church bars American combat in Indochina, so a track has no American strike to cue; the Republic owns no aircraft and no exploitation cell. The war was decided by aid cuts, fuel and ammunition, and by Thieu's order to abandon the Highlands, which no radar touches.

## §4.H Historical comparison

Against history, the cumulative force of three memoranda has changed the interior of the war and its record, and has not changed its outline. Concretely:

- **The sea route.** Closed to the II Corps and Delta coasts from mid-1964, about a year before Market Time closed it historically, with four trawlers intercepted and six turned back in TIDE TABLE's first six months alone; the Vung Ro landing of February 1965 does not happen. The price is Sihanoukville open from June 1965, about eighteen months early, beyond reach until 1970.
- **The Gulf of Tonkin.** No retaliation strikes on 5 August 1964; two US aircraft and one airman not lost and one not captured that day; a resolution passed on the real attack of 2 August, three days later and in the same words; and no 4 August story for the credibility gap to grow from.
- **Air losses over the North.** Fewer, from Spring High in July 1965 to Linebacker in 1972, by an electronic order of battle built before the strike forces needed it. I estimate perhaps 10–15 of Linebacker's 134 losses avoided and a similar proportion in Rolling Thunder's later years, and I hold the estimate loosely.
- **The record of the interdiction war.** Measured from 1965 rather than reconstructed after 1970: the Trail by the panhandle watch, Sihanoukville by the count, the North's roads by the Gulf watch, Commando Hunt by a second ledger. The claim of more trucks destroyed than existed never survives a season.
- **The incursion of 1970.** The caches found in ten days rather than six weeks, and the haul about a tenth larger.
- **The armour of 1972.** About 20–30 vehicles destroyed at the Ben Hai crossings under cloud that grounded history's air support, and somewhat higher PAVN losses at An Loc.
- **Lives.** Some American aircrew over the North and in the panhandle, some CIDG at Lang Vei, some ARVN at An Loc. I put no total on it; the honest sum is in the hundreds, not the thousands, and spread over eight years¹⁴⁵.

What has not changed is every main-force battle under the canopy, the war in the hamlets, the coups, Tet's political verdict, the drawdown, the Easter Offensive's territorial result, and the fall of Saigon on its date. The margin over history is real and concentrated in knowledge; it is not a margin in the war's result.

## §4.N Net assessment and end state

The end state does not move. The Republic of Vietnam falls on 30 April 1975, on the historical timeline: rung (a), collapse. These two provisions are reconnaissance aircraft, American-owned and American-operated, and every effect they have runs through a decision someone else makes: a Vietnamese Navy captain who steams, a President who holds or orders a strike, a corps commander who believes a warning or does not. Where those decisions were sound they improved the war at the margin; where they were not, nothing that flies at 60,000 feet repaired them.

Rung (b), dependent survival, is not reachable on this force because the Republic's collapse in 1975 followed the departure of the Americans and the cutting of aid, and neither the land and police reforms of the first two memoranda nor these aircraft supply what the South lacked once the Americans left: a conventional army able to stop a PAVN offensive without American air power, and an American government willing to send that air power back. The first two memoranda moved the political deficit where the government held the night; this one moves the intelligence deficit about the enemy's rear. The conventional deficit and the American one are untouched. The distance to (b) is the same as it was after Memorandum 002.

I propose no redraw of the phases. The war's shape has not outgrown them; its record has improved within them.

## §5 Delta ledger

- **Forces gained:** 2 U-2S Dragon Lady (Block 20) with ASARS-2A, SYERS-2C and ASIP, and their ground exploitation station, at Bien Hoa from 1 January 1964; 4 RQ-4B Global Hawk Block 40 with MP-RTIP, a Launch and Recovery Element at Clark and a Mission Control Element at Tan Son Nhut, from 1 January 1964.
- **Forces lost:** none. One U-2S out of service for seven weeks after the mortar attack of 1 November 1964.
- **Personnel:** about 600 spaces, about 220 for the U-2S and about 380 for the Global Hawks, against no ceiling¹⁰⁵.
- **Variables moved, at phase ends:** finding the main force +1 in Phases I–V (from 0). No other headline variable moves a band. Sea infiltration falls by one band from 1964, as the manifest pushes, and is displaced to Cambodia; tonnage arriving is unchanged by band.
- **Events:** five new (TIDE TABLE, the Sihanoukville route, STEADY COUNT, the Sihanoukville count, the halt measured) and thirteen historical events altered, across Phases I–V. Phase VI unchanged.
- **New categories:** strategic and persistent airborne reconnaissance, the theater's first (isr).
- **End state:** unchanged, rung (a).

## §6 Changed / not changed

### What has changed

- **The sea route to the South's coast is closed from 1964**, by a radar that saw every trawler and a navy that knew where to steam. It is the cleanest effect in the memorandum, and it is bought at the price of Sihanoukville opening eighteen months early.
- **The war begins on a true attack.** No Pierce Arrow, no 4 August story, and a resolution resting on the attack that happened. Of all the things in this memorandum, this one acts closest to the root of the political clock.
- **The enemy's rear is measured, not guessed, from 1965.** The Trail, the North's roads and the Cambodian route are counted by the enemy's own movement, and every argument about interdiction and sanctuary is made from evidence five years earlier than history made it. This is the change I would least want to give back.
- **The air war loses fewer aircraft**, from Spring High to Linebacker, flying against an electronic order of battle built before it was needed, and its damage assessment is honest by the next morning.
- **Washington learns its failures sooner**: the fuel strikes in weeks rather than months, the truck claims in a season rather than after the war. Bad news delivered early is the kind that buys time.
- **The armour of 1972 is struck in the cloud**, and Lang Vei and An Loc cost the defenders somewhat less.

### What has not changed

- **The main force under the canopy.** Not one main-force battle of the war is changed in its course. The enemy still chooses when to fight, and these aircraft give a better answer to where his headquarters is this week and none to where his battalions are tonight.
- **The war in the hamlets.** The infrastructure is untouched by anything these aircraft do; that half of the war belongs to the police and the land programme of the first two memoranda.
- **What arrives in the South.** Interdiction, now honestly measured, still stops a fraction of a tonnage that exceeds the enemy's requirement.
- **Washington's control of the air war**, which the better pictures tightened rather than loosened, and the decisions that pictures could not change: the fuel strikes, the refusal to strike the SA-2 sites, the naming of COSVN, the timing of Lam Son 719.
- **Tet's political verdict and the drawdown's pace**, and the fall of Saigon on 30 April 1975.
- **The Republic's own capability.** The aircraft and their analysts leave with the Americans; nothing transfers.

### Trajectory Log entry

Memorandum 003 gave this command two U-2S and four Global Hawks on the war's first day, and with them the first honest picture of the enemy's rear. The war's record moved; its outcome did not. The coastal radar closed the trawler route in 1964 and pushed the enemy's southern supply into Sihanoukville eighteen months early. On 4 August 1964 the Gulf watch saw no torpedo boats, Pierce Arrow was never flown, and the war's only legislative authority rested on the real attack of 2 August. From 1965 the Trail, the North's roads and the Cambodian route were counted rather than guessed, so the bombing's failures and the inflated truck claims reached Washington in weeks rather than years. The radar mapped the 1970 incursion's caches and struck the 1972 armour through cloud, but it deepened the fixation on Khe Sanh before Tet, saw nothing under the canopy or in the hamlets, and left with the Americans. Saigon still falls on 30 April 1975, at rung (a).

### Positions recorded

- **POS003-1** · vars: FIND.main_force, FIX.capability, STRIKE.responsiveness · Airborne radar and signals reconnaissance find the enemy's movement in the open and his headquarters' transmitters, never his regiments under canopy; their value in any engagement equals what can be put on a track within the hour, and an improvement in finding is never claimed as an improvement in fixing (003 §2 F003-5; §4.P2–P5)
- **POS003-2** · vars: INFIL.sea, SANCT.cambodia, TRAIL.tonnage_arriving · In this war the sea route to the South's coast closed in 1964 under observation and the enemy's southern supply moved to Sihanoukville from June 1965; supply through Cambodia, not by sea, is the principal external line into III and IV Corps from 1965 until Lon Nol's coup (003 §2 F003-3; EV003-2)
- **POS003-3** · vars: US.political_tolerance, US.credibility_gap · In this war no attack of 4 August 1964 was claimed and Pierce Arrow was not flown; the Southeast Asia Resolution rests on the attack of 2 August, and the Tonkin deception is not part of the credibility gap in any later phase (003 §4.P1, §4.P3; EV000-3)

### Persons recorded

- **Harold Kessling** · Major, later Lieutenant Colonel, USAF; chief of the radar and imagery exploitation cell at Tan Son Nhut from 1964 · invented in 003 · Had the watch on the night of 4 August 1964 and reported no boats near the destroyers; built the Sihanoukville count night by night from 1965 to the joint estimate of March 1967
